Precise Subtyping for Asynchronous Multiparty Sessions (Extended Version) CCS: Theory of computation Process calculi CCS: Theory of computation Type structures
Silvia Ghilezan, Jovanka Pantović, Ivan Prokić, Alceste Scalas, Nobuko Yoshida

Abstract
. Session subtyping is a cornerstone of refinement of communicating processes: a process implementing a session type (i.e., a communication protocol) T T can be safely used whenever a process implementing one of its supertypes T ′ T^{\prime} is expected, in any context, without introducing deadlocks nor other communication errors. As a consequence, whenever T ⩽ T ′ T\leqslant T^{\prime} holds, it is safe to replace an implementation of T ′ T^{\prime} with an implementation of the subtype T T , which may allow for more optimised communication patterns. This paper presents the first formalisation of the precise subtyping relation for asynchronous multiparty sessions. We show that our subtyping relation is sound (i.e., guarantees safe process replacement, as outlined above) and also complete : any extension of the relation is unsound. To achieve our results, we develop a novel session decomposition technique, from full session types (including internal/external choices) into single input/output session trees (without choices). Previous work studies precise subtyping for binary sessions (with just two participants), or multiparty sessions (with any number of participants) and synchronous interaction. Here, we cover multiparty sessions with asynchronous interaction, where messages are transmitted via FIFO queues (as in the TCP/IP protocol), and prove that our subtyping is both operationally and denotationally precise. In the asynchronous multiparty setting, finding the precise subtyping relation is a highly complex task: this is because, under some conditions, participants can permute the order of their inputs and outputs, by sending some messages earlier or receiving some later, without causing errors; the precise subtyping relation must capture all such valid permutations — and consequently, its formalisation, reasoning and proofs become challenging. Our session decomposition technique overcomes this complexity, expressing the subtyping relation as a composition of refinement relations between single input/output trees, and providing a simple reasoning principle for asynchronous message optimisations.



1.
Introduction

Modern software systems are routinely designed and developed as ensembles of concurrent and distributed components, interacting via message-passing according to pre-determined
communication protocols
. A key challenge lies in ensuring that each component abides by the desired protocol, thus avoiding run-time failures due to, e.g., communication errors and deadlocks. One of the most successful approaches to this problem are
session types

(
Takeuchi
et al. 1994
;
Honda
et al. 1998
)
.
In their original formulation,
session types allow formalising two-party protocols
(e.g., for client-server interaction),
whose structure includes sequencing, choices, and recursion;
they were later extended to

multiparty
protocols
(
Honda
et al. 2008
;
Honda
et al. 2016
)
.
By describing
(multiparty) protocols as types
, session types provide a type-based methodology to statically verify
whether a given process implements a desired protocol.
Beyond the theoretical developments, multiparty session types have been implemented in mainstream programming languages
such as Java, Python, Go, Scala, C, TypeScript, F
\sharp
,
OCaml, Haskell, Erlang
(
Ancona
et al. 2016
;
Gay and Ravara 2017
)
.

Precise subtyping

The substitution
principle of
Liskov and Wing 1994
establishes a general notion of
subtyping
: if
T
is subtype of
T^{\prime}
, then
an object of type
T
can always replace an object of type
T^{\prime}
,
in any context.
Similar notions arise in the realm of process calculi, since
Pierce and
Sangiorgi 1996
first introduced
IO-subtyping for input and output channel capabilities in the
\pi
-calculus. As session types are protocols,
the notion of
session subtyping

(first introduced by
Gay and Hole 2005
) can be interpreted as
protocol refinement
:
given two types/protocols
T
and
T^{\prime}
, if
T
is a subtype (or refinement) of
T^{\prime}
, then a process that implements
T
can be used whenever a process implementing
T^{\prime}
is needed. In general, when a type system is equipped with a subtyping (subsumption) rule,
then we can enlarge the set of typable programs
by enlarging its subtyping relation.
On the one hand, a larger subtyping can be desirable,
since it makes the type system more flexible,
and the verification more powerful;
however, a subtyping relation that is
too
large
makes the type system unsound:
e.g., if we consider
\mathtt{string}
as a subtype of
\mathtt{real}
,
then expressions like
1+\mathtt{``foo"}
become typable,
and typed programs can crash at run-time. Finding the “right subtyping” (not too strict, nor too lax)
leads to the problem of finding a canonical,
precise
subtyping
for a given type system — i.e., a subtyping relation that is sound
(“typed programs never go wrong”) and cannot be further enlarged
(otherwise, the type system would become unsound).
The problem has been widely studied for the
\lambda
-calculus
(
Blackburn et al. 2012
;
Ligatti
et al. 2017
;
Dezani-Ciancaglini and Ghilezan 2014
;
Dezani-Ciancaglini et al. 1998
)
; several papers have also tackled the problem in the realm of session types

(
Chen et al. 2017
;
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
.
A session subtyping relation
\leqslant
is
precise
when it is both
sound
and
complete
:

soundness:

means that, if we have a context
C
expecting some process
P^{\prime}
of type
T^{\prime}
, then
T\leqslant T^{\prime}
implies that any process
P
of type
T
can be placed into
C
without causing “bad behaviours” (e.g., communication errors or deadlocks);

completeness:

means that
\leqslant
cannot be extended without becoming unsound. More accurately: if
T\not\leqslant T^{\prime}
, then we can find a process
P
of type
T
, and a context
C
expecting a process of type
T^{\prime}
, such that if we place
P
in
C
, it will cause “bad behaviours.”

Asynchronous Multiparty Session Subtyping

This work tackles the problem of finding
the precise subtyping relation
\leqslant
for
multiparty asynchronous
session types. The starting point is a type system for
processes that

(1)

implement types/protocols with 2 or more participants, and

(2)

communicate through a medium that buffers messages while preserving their order — as in TCP/IP sockets,
and akin to the original papers on multiparty session types

(
Honda
et al. 2008
;
Bettini et al. 2008
)
.

For example, consider a scenario where participant
{\sf r}
waits for the outcome of a computation from
{\sf p}
, and notifies
{\sf q}
on whether to continue the calculation (we omit part of the system “
\cdots
”):

\begin{array}[]{c}{\sf r}\triangleleft P_{\sf r}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft P_{\sf p}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf q}\triangleleft P_{\sf q}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;\cdots\\[5.69054pt]
\text{where }\;P_{\sf r}\;=\;\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathsf{if}\penalty\ (x>\texttt{0})\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathit{fatal}).\mathsf{if}\penalty\ (\neg\mathit{fatal})\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}\langle 42\rangle.\mathbf{0}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}

Above,
{\sf r}\triangleleft P_{\sf r}
denotes a process
P_{\sf r}
executed by participant
{\sf r}
,
{{\sf p}}?{\ell(x)}
is an input of message
\ell
with payload value
x
from participant
{\sf p}
, and
{{\sf q}}!{\ell}\langle{5}\rangle
is an output of message
\ell
with payload
5
to participant
{\sf q}
. In the example,
{\sf r}
waits to receive either
\mathit{{success}}
or
\mathit{{error}}
from
{\sf p}
. In case of
\mathit{{success}}
,
{\sf r}
checks whether the message payload
x

is greater than
0
(zero), and tells
{\sf q}
to either
\mathit{{cont}}
inue
(forwarding the payload
x
) or
\mathit{{stop}}
, then terminates (
\mathbf{0}
); in case of
\mathit{{error}}
,
{\sf r}
checks whether the error is non-fatal, and then tells
{\sf q}
to either
\mathit{{cont}}
inue
(with a constant value
42
), or
\mathit{{stop}}
. Note that
{\sf r}
is blocked until a message is sent by
{\sf p}
, and correspondingly,
{\sf q}
is waiting for
{\sf r}
, who is waiting for
{\sf p}
.
Yet, depending on the application, one might attempt to
locally optimise

{\sf r}
, by replacing
P_{\sf r}
above with the process:

P^{\prime}_{\sf r}\;\;=\;\;\mathsf{if}\penalty\ (\ldots)\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}\langle 42\rangle.\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(y).\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}\langle\rangle.\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(y).\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}

Process
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
internally decides (with an omitted condition “
\ldots
”) whether to tell
{\sf q}
to
\mathit{{cont}}
inue with a constant value
42
, or
\mathit{{stop}}
. Then,
{\sf r}
receives the
\mathit{{success}}
/
\mathit{{error}}
message
from
{\sf p}
, and does nothing with it. As a result,
{\sf q}
can start its computation immediately, without waiting for
{\sf p}
. Observe that this optimisation permutes the order of inputs and outputs in the process of
{\sf r}
: is it “correct”? I.e., could this permutation introduce any deadlock or communication error in the system? Do we have enough information to determine it, or do we need to know the behaviour of
P_{\sf p}
and
P_{\sf q}
, and the omitted part (“
\cdots
”) of the system? If this optimisation never causes deadlocks or communication errors,
then a session subtyping relation should allow for it: i.e., if
T^{\prime}
is the type of
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
, and
T
is the type of
P_{\sf r}
, we should have
T^{\prime}\leqslant T
— hence, the type system should let
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
be used in place of
P_{\sf r}
. (We illustrate such types later on, in Example
3.9
.) Due to practical needs, similar program optimisations have been implemented
for various programming languages
(
Ng
et al. 2012
;
Ng
et al. 2015
;
Hu 2017
;
Castro-Perez and
Yoshida 2020b
;
Castro-Perez and
Yoshida 2020a
;
Yoshida et al. 2008
)
. Yet, this optimisation is
not
allowed by
synchronous
multiparty session subtyping
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
: in fact, under synchronous communication, there are cases where replacing
P_{\sf r}
with
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}

would introduce deadlocks. However, most real-world distributed and concurrent systems use
asynchronous
communication: does asynchrony make the optimisation above
always
safe, and should the subtyping allow for it? If we prove that this optimisation, and others, are indeed sound under asynchrony, it would be possible to check them locally, at the type-level, for each individual participant in a multiparty session.

Contributions

We present the
first formalisation of the
precise
subtyping
relation
\leqslant
for multiparty asynchronous session types
. We introduce the relation (
Section

3.2
)
and prove that it is
operationally
precise
(
Theorem

5.13
),
i.e., satisfies the notions of “soundness” and
“completeness” outlined above. Then, we use this result as a
stepping stone to prove that
\leqslant
is also

denotationally precise
(
Theorem

7.2
),
and
precise wrt. liveness
(
Theorem

7.3
).

A key element of our contribution is a novel approach based on

session decomposition
: given two session types
T
and
T^{\prime}
, we formalise the subtyping

T\leqslant T^{\prime}
as a composition of refinement relations
\lesssim
over

single-input, single-output (SISO) trees
extracted from
T
and

T^{\prime}
. (The idea and its motivation are explained in
Section

3.1
.)

We base our development on a recent advancement of
the multiparty session types theory
(
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
)
: this way, we achieve not only a precise subtyping relation,
but also a simpler formulation, and more general results than previous work
on multiparty session subtyping
(
Chen et al. 2017
;
Mostrous and
Yoshida 2015
;
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
, supporting the verification of a larger set of concurrent and distributed
processes and protocols.

To demonstrate the tractability and generality of our subtyping
relation, we discuss various examples — including one that is

not
supported by a sound algorithm for asynchronous

binary
session subtyping (
Example

3.10
), and
another where we prove the correctness of a messaging optimisation
(based on
double buffering

(
Huang
et al. 2002
, Section 3.2)
,
adapted from
(
Mostrous
et al. 2009
;
Castro-Perez and
Yoshida 2020a
;
Yoshida et al. 2008
)
) applied to a
distributed data processing scenario (
Section

6
).

Overview

Section
2
formalises the asynchronous multiparty session calculus.
Section
3
presents our asynchronous multiparty session subtyping
relation, with its decomposition technique.
Section
4
introduces the typing system, proving its soundness. Section
5
proves the completeness and preciseness of our subtyping.
Section
6
applies our subtyping
to prove the correctness of an asynchronous
optimisation of a distributed system.
Section
7
proves additional preciseness results:
denotational preciseness, and preciseness wrt. liveness.
Related work is in Section
8
. Proofs and additional examples
are available in the appendices.

2.
Asynchronous Multiparty Session Calculus

This section formalises the syntax and operational semantics of an
asynchronous multiparty session calculus. Our formulation is a streamlined presentation of the session calculus
by
Bettini et al. 2008
,
omitting some elements (in particular, session creation and shared channels)
to better focus on subtyping.
The same design is adopted, e.g., by
Ghilezan et al. 2019

— but here we include message queues,
for asynchronous (FIFO-based) communication.

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{v}\;\Coloneqq\;\mathsf{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{true}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{false}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {()}\qquad{\mathsf{i}\;\Coloneqq\;{\texttt{0}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{n}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ -\mathsf{n}}\qquad{\mathsf{n}\;\Coloneqq\;1\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{n}+1}\\
\mathsf{e}\;\Coloneqq\;x\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{v}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf succ}(\mathsf{e})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf inv}(\mathsf{e})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \neg{\mathsf{e}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\mathsf{e}>\texttt{0}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{e}\approx()}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}rcll@{}}\mathcal{M}&\Coloneqq&&\text{\bf Sessions}\\
&&{\sf p}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h&\text{individual participant}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&\mathcal{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}&\text{parallel}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&\mathsf{error}&\text{error}\\[5.69054pt]
h&\Coloneqq&&\text{\bf Message queues}\\
&&\varnothing&\text{empty queue}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&\left({\sf q},\ell(\mathsf{v})\right)&\text{message}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&h\cdot h&\text{concatenation}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{@{}rcll@{}}P,Q&\Coloneqq&\text{\bf Processes}\\
&&\sum_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).P_{i}&\text{external choice}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&{\sf p}!\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P&\text{output}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ Q&\text{conditional}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&X&\text{variable}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&\mu X.P&\text{recursion}\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}&\mathbf{0}&\text{inaction}\end{array}

Table 1
.
Syntax of values (
\mathsf{v}
), expressions (
\mathsf{e}
), sessions, processes, and queues.

2.1.
Syntax

The syntax of our calculus is defined in Table
1
.
Values and expressions are standard:
a
value

\mathsf{v}
can be
an integer
\mathsf{i}
(positive
\mathsf{n}
, negative
-\mathsf{n}
, or zero
0
)
, a boolean
\mathsf{true}
/
\mathsf{false}
,
or unit
()
(that we will often omit, for brevity);
an
expression

\mathsf{e}
can be a
variable, a value, or a term built from expressions by applying the
operators
{\tt succ},{\tt inv},\neg
, or the relations
>,\approx
.

Asynchronous multiparty sessions

(ranged over by
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\ldots
)
are parallel compositions of individual
participants
(ranged over by
{\sf p},{\sf q},\ldots
) associated with their own
process

P
and
message queue

h
(notation:
{\sf p}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h
). In the processes syntax, the
external choice

\sum_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).P_{i}
denotes the input from participant
{\sf p}
of a message with label
\ell_{i}
carrying value
x_{i}
, for any
i\!\in\!I
;
instead,
{\sf p}!\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P
denotes the
output
towards participant
{\sf p}
of a message with label
\ell
carrying the value returned by expression
\mathsf{e}
.
The
conditional

\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ Q
is standard. The term
{\sf p}\triangleleft h
states that
h
is the
output message queue
of participant
{\sf p}
; if a message
({\sf q},\ell(\mathsf{v}))
is in the queue of participant
{\sf p}
, it means that
{\sf p}
has sent
\ell(\mathsf{v})
to
{\sf q}
.
1
1

1

Alternatively, we could formalise a calculus
with actor-style
input
message queues,
at the cost of some additional notation;
the semantics would be equivalent
(
Demangeon and
Yoshida 2015
)
and would not influence subtyping.

Messages are consumed by their recipients on a FIFO (first in, first out) basis.
The rest of the syntax is standard
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
.
We assume that in recursive processes, recursion variables are guarded by external choices and/or outputs.

We also define the set
\mathtt{act}(P)
,
containing the
input and output actions
of
P
;
its elements have the form
{\sf p}?
or
{\sf p}!
,
representing an input or an output from/to participant
{\sf p}
, respectively:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathtt{act}(\mathbf{0})=\emptyset\qquad\mathtt{act}({\sf p}!\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P)=\{{\sf p}!\}\cup\mathtt{act}(P)\qquad\mathtt{act}(\sum_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).P_{i})=\{{\sf p}?\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathtt{act}(P_{i})\\
\mathtt{act}(\mu X.P^{\prime})=\mathtt{act}(P^{\prime})\qquad\mathtt{act}(\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P_{1}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ P_{2})=\mathtt{act}(P_{1})\cup\mathtt{act}(P_{2})\end{array}

2.2.
Reductions and Errors

First, we give the operational semantics of expressions.
To this purpose, we define an
evaluation context

\mathcal{E}

as an expression (from
Table

1
) with exactly one hole
[\,]
, given by the grammar:

\mathcal{E}\;\Coloneqq\;[\,]\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf succ}(\mathcal{E})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf inv}(\mathcal{E})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \neg{\mathcal{E}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{E}>\texttt{0}

We write
\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{e})
for the expression obtained from
context
\mathcal{E}
by filling its unique hole with
\mathsf{e}
.
The
value of an expression
is computed as defined in Table
4
:
the notation
\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v}
means that expression
\mathsf{e}
evaluates to
\mathsf{v}
.
Notice that the successor operation
{\tt succ}
is
defined on natural numbers (i.e. positive integers), the inverse operation
{\tt inv}
is
defined on integers, and negation
\neg
is
defined on boolean values;
moreover, the evaluation of
\mathsf{e}>\texttt{0}
is defined only if
\mathsf{e}
evaluates to some integer, while the evaluation of
\mathsf{e}\approx()
is defined only if
\mathsf{e}
is exactly the unit value.

The operational semantics of our calculus
is defined in Table
4
.
By
\text{\scriptsize[{r-send}]},
a participant
{\sf p}
sends
\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle
to a participant
{\sf q}
, enqueuing the
message
({\sf q},\ell(\mathsf{v}))
.
Rule
[
r-rcv
]
lets participant
{\sf p}
receive a message from
{\sf q}
: if one of the input labels
\ell_{k}
matches a queued message
({\sf p},\ell_{k}(\mathsf{v}))
previously sent by
{\sf q}
(for some
k\in I
), the message is dequeued, and the continuation
P_{k}
proceeds with value
\mathsf{v}
substituting
x_{k}
. The rules for conditionals are standard.
Rule
[
r-struct
]
defines
the reduction modulo a standard
structural congruence

\equiv
, defined in Table
4
.

\small\begin{array}[t]{@{}c@{}}{\sf succ}(\mathsf{n})\downarrow(\mathsf{n}+1)\hskip 18.49988pt{{\sf inv}(\mathsf{n})\downarrow-\mathsf{n}\hskip 18.49988pt{\sf inv}(-\mathsf{n})\downarrow\mathsf{n}\hskip 18.49988pt{\sf inv}(\texttt{0})\downarrow\texttt{0}}\\
\neg\mathsf{true}\downarrow\mathsf{false}\hskip 18.49988pt\neg\mathsf{false}\downarrow\mathsf{true}\hskip 18.49988pt{\big(()\approx()\big)\downarrow\mathsf{true}}\hskip 18.49988pt\mathsf{v}\downarrow\mathsf{v}\\[5.69054pt]
{(\mathsf{e}_{1}>\texttt{0})\downarrow\mathsf{true}\mathsf{e}_{1}\downarrow\mathsf{n}\hskip 18.49988pt(\mathsf{e}_{1}>\texttt{0})\downarrow\mathsf{false}\mathsf{e}_{1}\downarrow-\mathsf{n}\hskip 18.49988pt(\mathsf{e}_{1}>\texttt{0})\downarrow\mathsf{false}\mathsf{e}_{1}\downarrow\texttt{0}}\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{e})\downarrow\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{v})\downarrow\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\end{array}

Table 2
.
Evaluation rules for expressions.

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}llr@{}}\text{\scriptsize[{r-send}]}&{{\sf p}\triangleleft{\sf q}!\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h_{{\sf p}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\longrightarrow\;{\sf p}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h_{\sf p}\cdot({\sf q},\ell(\mathsf{v}))\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}}&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v})\\[2.84526pt]

&\hskip 91.04872pt\longrightarrow\;{\sf p}\triangleleft P_{k}\{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{v}}}{{x_{k}}}\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h_{\sf p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{r-cond-T}]}&{\sf p}\triangleleft\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\longrightarrow{\sf p}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{true})\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{r-cond-F}]}&{\sf p}\triangleleft\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\longrightarrow{\sf p}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{false})\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{r-struct}]}&\mathcal{M}_{1}\equiv\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{M}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\implies\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{M}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{2}\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{err-mism}]}&{\sf p}\triangleleft\sum_{i\in I}{\sf q}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).P_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h_{\sf p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft({\sf p},\ell(\mathsf{v}))\cdot h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathsf{error}&(\forall i\in I.\ell_{i}\neq\ell)\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{err-ophn}]}&{\sf p}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h_{\sf p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft({\sf p},\ell(\mathsf{v}))\cdot h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathsf{error}&({{\sf q}?}\not\in\mathtt{act}(P))\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{err-strv}]}&{\sf p}\triangleleft\sum_{i\in I}{\sf q}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).P_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h_{\sf p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf q}\triangleleft h_{\sf q}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathsf{error}&\hskip-113.81102pt({{\sf p}!}\not\in\mathtt{act}(Q),h_{\sf q}\not\equiv({\sf p},-(-))\cdot h_{\sf q}^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{err-eval}]}&{{\sf p}\triangleleft\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathsf{error}}&(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{\not{\downarrow}}\mathsf{true}\;\text{ and }\;\mathsf{e}\mathbin{\not{\downarrow}}\mathsf{false})\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{err-eval2}]}&{{\sf p}\triangleleft{\sf q}!\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft h\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathsf{error}}&({\not\exists\mathsf{v}:\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v}})\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{err-dlock}]}&{\prod_{j\in J}\left({\sf p}_{j}\triangleleft\sum_{i_{j}\in I_{j}}{\sf q}_{j}?\ell_{i_{j}}(x_{i_{j}}).P_{i_{j}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}_{j}\triangleleft h_{{\sf p}_{j}}\right)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \prod_{j\in J^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}_{j}\triangleleft h_{{\sf p}_{j}}\right)}&{\hskip-36.98866pt(\forall j\!\in\!J\!\neq\!\emptyset:\forall i\!\in\!J\!\cup\!J^{\prime}:}\\
&\hskip 91.04872pt\longrightarrow\mathsf{error}&{h_{{\sf p}_{i}}\!\not\equiv\!({\sf p}_{j},-(-))\cdot h^{\prime}_{{\sf p}_{i}})}\end{array}

Table 3
.
Reduction relation on sessions.

Structural congruence rules for queues, processes, and sessions.

Table
4
also formalises
error reductions
, modelling the following scenarios: in
[
err-mism
]
,
a process tries to read a queued message with an unsupported label;
in
[
err-orph
]
, there is a queued message from
{\sf q}
to
{\sf p}
, but
{\sf p}
’s process does not contain any input from
{\sf q}
,
hence the message is orphan;
in
[
err-strv
]
,

{\sf p}
is waiting for a message from
{\sf q}
, but no such message is queued, and
{\sf q}
’s process does not contain any output for
{\sf p}
, hence
{\sf p}
will starve;
in
[
err-eval
]
, a condition does not evaluate to a boolean value;
in
[
err-eval2
]
, an expression like “
{\sf succ}(\mathsf{true})
” cannot reduce to any value;
in
[
err-dlock
]
,
the session cannot reduce further, but at least one participant is expecting an input.

Example 2.1 (Reduction relation).

We now describe the operational semantics using the example from the Introduction. Consider the session:

\small{\sf r}\triangleleft\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathsf{if}\penalty\ (x>\texttt{0})\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathit{fatal}).\mathsf{if}\penalty\ (\neg\mathit{fatal})\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}\langle 42\rangle.\mathbf{0}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft P_{\sf p}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft\varnothing\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf q}\triangleleft P_{\sf q}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;\cdots

In this session, the process of
{\sf r}
is blocked until a message is sent by
{\sf p}
to
{\sf r}
. If this cannot happen (e.g., because
P_{\sf p}
is
\mathbf{0}
),
the session will reduce to
\mathsf{error}
by
[
err-starv
]
. Now, consider the session above optimised with the process:

{\sf r}\triangleleft\mathsf{if}\penalty\ (\mathsf{e})\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}\langle 42\rangle.\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(y).\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}\langle\rangle.\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(y).\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}

This session could reduce to
\mathsf{error}
if
\mathsf{e}

does
not
evaluate to
\mathsf{true}
or
\mathsf{false}
(by
[
err-eval
]
).
Instead, if
\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{true}
, then
{\sf r}
can fire
[
r-cond-T
]

and
[
r-send
]
, reducing the session to:

{\sf r}\triangleleft{\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(y).\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft({\sf q},\mathit{{cont}}{(42)})\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft P_{\sf p}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft\varnothing\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf q}\triangleleft P_{\sf q}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;\cdots

Otherwise, if
\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{false}
, then
{\sf r}
can fire
[
r-cond-F
]

and
[
r-send
]
, reducing the session to:

{\sf r}\triangleleft{\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(x).\mathbf{0}\\
{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(y).\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right\}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft({\sf q},\mathit{{stop}}{()})\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft P_{\sf p}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf p}\triangleleft\varnothing\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;{\sf q}\triangleleft P_{\sf q}\;\penalty\ |\penalty\ \;\cdots

In both cases,
{\sf r}
reduces to
\mathbf{0}
(by
[
r-rcv
]
)
if it receives a
\mathit{{success}}
/
\mathit{{error}}
message from
{\sf p}
; meanwhile, if
{\sf q}
is ready to receive an input from
{\sf p}
,
then
{\sf q}
can continue by consuming a message from
{\sf p}
’s output queue.
This kind of optimisation will be verified by means of subtyping in the following section.

3.
Multiparty Session Types and Asynchronous Subtyping

This section formalises multiparty session types,
and introduces our asynchronous session subtyping relation. We begin with the standard definition of (local) session types.

Definition 3.1.

The
sorts

{\mathsf{S}}
and
session types

{\mathbb{T}}
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\hskip 0.8cm}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\mathsf{S}}&\Coloneqq&\mathtt{nat}\!\!\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \!\!\mathtt{int}\!\!\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \!\!\mathtt{bool}\!\!\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \!\!\mathtt{unit}\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt&{\mathbb{T}}&\Coloneqq&\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathbb{T}}_{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathbb{T}}_{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathtt{end}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \textbf{t}\end{array}

with
I\!\neq\!\emptyset
,
and
\forall i,j\!\in\!I{:}\;i\!\neq\!j\Rightarrow\ell_{i}\!\neq\!\ell_{j}
. We assume guarded recursion. We define
\equiv
as the least congruence such that
\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}\equiv{\mathbb{T}}\{\nicefrac{{\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}}}{{\textbf{t}}}\}
.
We define
\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{T}})
as the set of participants occurring in
{\mathbb{T}}
.

Sorts are the types of values (
\mathtt{nat}
urals,
\mathtt{int}
egers,
\mathtt{bool}
eans, …).
A session type
{\mathbb{T}}
describes the behaviour of a participant in a multiparty session.
The
branching type
(or
external choice
)
\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathbb{T}}_{i}
denotes waiting for a message from participant
{\sf p}
,
where (for some
i\in I
) the message has label
\ell_{i}
and carries a payload value of sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{i}
; then, the interaction continues by following
{\mathbb{T}}_{i}
.
The
selection type
(or
internal choice
)
\tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathbb{T}}_{i}
denotes an output toward participant
{\sf p}
of a message with label
\ell_{i}
and payload of sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{i}
, after which the interaction follows
{\mathbb{T}}_{i}
(for some
i\in I
). Type
\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}
provides
recursion
, binding the
recursion variable

t
in
{\mathbb{T}}
; the guarded recursion assumption means: in
\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}
, we have
{\mathbb{T}}\not=\textbf{t}^{\prime}
for any
\textbf{t}^{\prime}
(which ensures
contractiveness
).
Type
\mathtt{end}
denotes that the participant has concluded its interactions.
For brevity, we often omit branch/selection symbols for singleton inputs/outputs, payloads of sort
\mathtt{unit}
,
unnecessary parentheses, and trailing
\mathtt{end}
s.

3.1.
Session Trees and Their Refinement

Our subtyping is defined in two phases:

(1)

we introduce a
refinement relation

\lesssim
for
session trees
having only singleton choices in all branchings and selections, called
single-input-single-output (SISO) trees

(definition below);

(2)

then, we consider trees that have only singleton choices in branchings (called
single-input (SI) trees
), or in selections (
single-output (SO) trees
), and we define the session subtyping
\leqslant
over all session types by considering their decomposition into SI, SO, and SISO trees.

This two-phases approach is crucial to capture all input/output reorderings needed by the
precise
subtyping relation, while taming the technical complexity of its formulation.
In essence, our session decomposition
is a “divide and conquer” technique to separately tackle the main
sources of complications in the definition of the subtyping, and in
the proofs of preciseness:

•

on the one hand, the SISO trees refinement
\lesssim
focuses on
capturing safe permutations and alterations of input/output messages,
that never cause deadlocks or communication errors under asynchrony;

•

on the other hand, the subtyping relation
\leqslant
focuses on
reconciling the SISO tree refinement
\lesssim
with the branching
structures (i.e., the choices) occurring in session types.

\textstyle{{\&{\sf p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{1}^{\mathsf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{1}^{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{2}^{\mathsf{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{bool}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{end}}
\textstyle{{\oplus{\sf q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{3}^{\mathsf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{3}^{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{4}^{\mathsf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{4}^{\mathsf{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{nat}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{int}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{end}}
\textstyle{{}_{\vdots}{\&{\sf p}}_{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{real}}

Session trees

To define our subtyping relation, we use (possibly infinite)
session trees
with the standard formulation of
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
, Appendix A.2)
, based on
Pierce 2002
. The diagram on the right depicts a session tree: its internal nodes represent branching (
\&{\sf p}
) or selection (
\oplus{\sf q}
) from/to a participant; leaf nodes are either payload sorts or
\mathtt{end}
; edge annotations are either
\ell^{\mathsf{P}}
or
\ell^{\mathsf{C}}
, respectively linking an internal node to the payload or continuation for message
\ell
. A type
{\mathbb{T}}
yields a tree
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)
:
the diagram above shows the (infinite) tree of the type
\mu\textbf{t}.\texternal\big\{{\sf p}?\ell_{1}(\mathtt{bool}).\tinternal\left\{{\sf q}!\ell_{3}(\mathtt{int}).\textbf{t},{\sf q}!\ell_{4}(\mathtt{real}).\mathtt{end}\right\}\,,\,{\sf p}?\ell_{2}(\mathtt{nat}).\mathtt{end}\big\}
.  Notably,
the tree of a recursive type
\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}
coincides with the tree of its unfolding
{\mathbb{T}}\{\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{T}}/\textbf{t}\}
. We will write
{\mathsf{T}}
to denote a session tree, and we will represent it using the
coinductive
syntax:

\textstyle{\mathsf{T}}\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;\mathtt{end}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{T}}_{i}

The above coinductive definition means that
{\mathsf{T}}
can be an infinite term, generated by infinite applications of the productions: this approach (previously adopted e.g. by
Castagna
et al. 2009b
) provides a compact way to represent (possibly infinite) trees.

SISO trees

A SISO tree
{\mathsf{W}}

only has singleton choices (i.e., one pair of payload+continuation edges) in all its branchings and selections. We represent
{\mathsf{W}}
with the
coinductive
syntax:

{\mathsf{W}}\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;\mathtt{end}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}

We will write
{\mathbb{W}}
to denote a SISO session type (i.e., having singleton choice in all its branchings and selections), such that
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}}\right)
yields a SISO tree.
We coinductively define the set
\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})
over a tree
{\mathsf{W}}
as the set of participant names together with actions
?
(input) or
!
(output), as:

\mathtt{act}(\mathtt{end})=\emptyset\qquad\mathtt{act}({\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})=\{{\sf p}?\}\cup\{\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})\}\qquad\mathtt{act}({\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})=\{{\sf p}!\}\cup\{\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})\}

By extension, we also define
\mathtt{act}({\mathbb{W}})=\mathtt{act}(\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}}\right))
.

SISO trees refinement

As discussed in
Section

1
, the asynchronous subtyping should support the reordering the input/output actions of a session type: the intuition is that, under certain conditions,
the subtype could
anticipate
some input/output actions
occurring in the supertype,
by performing them earlier than prescribed.
This is crucial to achieve the most flexible and
precise
subtyping.
More in detail, such reorderings can have two forms:

R1.

anticipating a branching from participant
{\sf p}
before a finite number of branchings which are
not
from
{\sf p}
;

R2.

anticipating a selection toward participant
{\sf p}
before a finite number of branchings (from any participant), and
also
before other selections which are
not
toward participant
{\sf p}
,

To characterise such reorderings of actions we define two kinds of
finite
sequences of inputs/outputs:

•

\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}
, containing only inputs from participants distinct from
{\sf p}
(we will use it to formalise reordering
R1
);

•

\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}
, containing inputs from any participant and/or outputs to participants distinct from
{\sf p}

(we will use it to formalise reordering
R2
).

Such sequences are inductively defined by the following productions:

\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}\Coloneqq{\sf q}?\ell({\mathsf{S}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf q}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}\qquad\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}\Coloneqq{\sf r}?\ell({\mathsf{S}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf r}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}\ \ ({\sf q}\!\neq\!{\sf p})

We will use the sequences
\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}
and
\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}

as prefixes for SISO trees;
notice that the base cases require the sequences to have at least one element.

Definition 3.2.

We define
subsorting

\leq\vcentcolon
as
the least reflexive binary relation on sorts (
Definition

3.1
)
such that
\mathtt{nat}\leq\vcentcolon\mathtt{int}
.
The
SISO tree refinement relation

\lesssim
is coinductively defined as:

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\hskip 18.49988pt{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})=\mathtt{act}(\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})=\mathtt{act}(\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{end}\lesssim\mathtt{end}\end{array}

Definition

3.2
above formalises a simulation between SISO trees,
as the largest relation closed backward under the given rules

(
Sangiorgi 2011
)
. Rule
[
ref-in
]
relates trees beginning with inputs from a same participant, and having equal message labels; the subtyping between carried sorts must be contravariant, and the continuation trees must be related.
Rule
[
ref-out
]
relates trees beginning with outputs to the same participant, and having equal message labels; the subtyping between carried sorts must be covariant, and the continuation trees must be related.
Rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]

captures reordering
R1
:
it enables anticipating an input from participant
{\sf p}
before a finite number of inputs from any other participant;
the two payload sorts and the rest of the trees satisfy the same conditions as in rule
[
ref-in
]
, while “
\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})=\mathtt{act}(\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
” ensures soundness: without such a condition, the tree refinement relation could “forget” some inputs.
(See
Example

3.5
below.)
Rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]

captures reordering
R2
:
it enables anticipating an output to participant
{\sf p}
before a finite number of inputs from any participant and/or
outputs to any other participant; the payload types and the rest of the two trees are related similarly to rule
[
ref-out
]
, while “
\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})=\mathtt{act}(\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
” ensures that inputs or outputs are not “forgotten,” similarly to rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]
.
(See
Example

3.5
below.)

Lemma 3.3.

The refinement relation
\lesssim
over SISO trees is reflexive and transitive.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

Example 3.4 (SISO tree refinement).

Consider the session types:

{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\;=\;\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\textbf{t}\qquad\qquad{\mathbb{W}}_{2}\;=\;\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}

Their trees are related by the following infinite coinductive derivation
(notice that the coinductive premise and conclusion coincide); we
highlight
the inputs matched by rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]
:

\small\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{1}}\right)\;=\;{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}})$}}.{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{1}}\right)\;\lesssim\;{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}})$}}.\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{2}}\right)\;=\;\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{2}}\right){\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{1}}\right)\;\lesssim\;{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{2}}\right)\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{1}}\right)\;\lesssim\;\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{W}}_{2}}\right)

An example using rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]
is available later on
(
Example

3.9
).

Example 3.5.

We now illustrate why we need the clauses on
\mathtt{act}(...)

in rules
[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]
and
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]
(
Definition

3.2
).
Consider the following session types:

{\mathbb{T}}\;=\;\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\qquad\qquad{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\;=\;{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathbb{T}}\;=\;{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}

Observe that
{\mathbb{T}}
is “forgetting” to perform the input
{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime})

occurring in
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
.

If we omit the clause on
\mathtt{act}(...)
in rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]
,
we can construct the following infinite coinductive derivation (notice that the coinductive premise and conclusion coincide;
we
highlight
the inputs matched by rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]
):

\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\;=\;{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}})$}}.\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\;\lesssim\;{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}})$}}.\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\;=\;\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\;\lesssim\;{\sf q}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\;=\;\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)

Were we to admit
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\lesssim\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)
,
then later on (
Definition

3.7
)
we would consider
{\mathbb{T}}
a subtype of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,
which would be unsound: in fact, this would allow our type system
(
Section

4
) to type-check processes
that “forget” to perform inputs and cause orphan message errors
(rule
[
err-ophn
]
in
Table

4
).

A similar example can be constructed for rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]
,
with the following session types:

{\mathbb{T}}\;=\;\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\qquad\qquad{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\;=\;{\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathbb{T}}\;=\;{\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}

Now,
{\mathbb{T}}
is “forgetting” to perform the output
{\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime})
.
If we omit the clause on
\mathtt{act}(...)
in rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]
,
we could derive
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\lesssim\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)

through an infinite sequence of instances of
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]

with
\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}={\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime})
;
were we to admit this, we would later be able to type-check processes
that “forget” to perform outputs
and cause starvation errors
(rule
[
err-strv
]
in
Table

4
).

Remark 3.6 (Prefixes vs.
n
-hole contexts).

The binary asynchronous subtyping by

Chen
et al. 2014
;
Chen et al. 2017
uses the
n
-hole branching type context

\mathcal{A}\Coloneqq[\ ]^{n}\!\!\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \!\!\&_{i\in I}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
,
which complicates the rules and reasoning
(see Fig.2, Fig.3 in
Chen et al. 2017
). Our
\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}
and
\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}
have a similar purpose, but they are simpler (just sequences of inputs or outputs), and cater for multiple participants.

SO trees and SI trees

To formalise our subtyping, we need two more kinds of session trees:
single-output (SO) trees
, denoted
{\mathsf{U}}
, have only singleton choices in their selections; dually,
single-input (SI) trees
, denoted
{\mathsf{V}}
, have only singleton branchings. We represent them with a
coinductive
syntax:

{\mathsf{U}}\;\Coloneqq\;\mathtt{end}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{U}}_{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{U}}\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 11.74988pt{\mathsf{V}}\;\Coloneqq\;\mathtt{end}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{V}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{V}}_{i}

We will write
{\mathbb{U}}
(resp.
{\mathbb{V}}
) to denote a SO (resp. SI) session type, i.e., with only singleton selections (resp. branchings), such that
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}}\right)
) yields a SO (resp. SI) tree.

We decompose session trees into their SO/SI subtrees, with the functions
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
/
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\llbracket\mathtt{end}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}&=&\{\mathtt{end}\}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\llbracket\tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}&=&\{{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{U}}:{\mathsf{U}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}},i\in I\}\\
\llbracket\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}&=&\{\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{U}}:{\mathsf{U}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\llbracket\mathtt{end}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}&=&\{\mathtt{end}\}\end{array}\qquad\ \begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\,\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\llbracket\tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}&=&\{\tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{V}}:{\mathsf{V}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\}\\
\llbracket\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}&=&\{{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathsf{V}}:{\mathsf{V}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}},i\in I\}\end{array}

Hence, when
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
is applied to a session tree
{\mathsf{T}}
, it gives the set of all SO trees obtained by taking only a single choice from each selection in
{\mathsf{T}}
(i.e., we take a single continuation edge and the corresponding payload edge starting in a selection node).
The function
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
is dual:
it takes a single selection from each branching in
{\mathsf{T}}
. Notice that for any SO tree
{\mathsf{U}}
, and SI tree
{\mathsf{V}}
, both
\llbracket{\mathsf{U}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
and
\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
yield SISO trees.
We will provide an example shortly (see
Example

3.9
below).

3.2.
Asynchronous Multiparty Session Subtyping

We can now define our asynchronous session subtyping
\leqslant
: it relates two session types by decomposing them into their SI, SO, and SISO trees, and checking their refinements.

Definition 3.7.

The
asynchronous subtyping relation
\leqslant
over session trees is defined as:

{\mathsf{T}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}\forall{\mathsf{U}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\quad\forall{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\quad\exists{\mathsf{W}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{U}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\quad\exists{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\qquad{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}

The subtyping relation for session types is defined as

{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
iff
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\leqslant\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)
.

Definition
3.7
says that a session tree
{\mathsf{T}}
is subtype of
{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}
if, for all
SO decompositions of
{\mathsf{T}}
and all SI decompositions of
{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}
, there are paths (i.e., SISO decompositions) related by
\lesssim
.

Lemma 3.8.

The asynchronous subtyping relation
\leqslant
is reflexive and transitive.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

We now illustrate the relation with two examples: we reprise the scenario in the introduction (
Example

3.9
), and we discuss a case from
(
Bravetti et al. 2019a
;
Bravetti et al. 2019b
)
(
Example

3.10
).

More examples are available in Appendix
A.1

Example 3.9.

Consider the opening example in Section
1
. The following types describe the interactions of
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
and
P_{\sf r}
, respectively:

\small{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}=\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.\hskip 10.80002pt{\mathbb{T}}=\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int}).}\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

In order to derive
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}
, we first show the two SO trees such that
\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}=\left\{{\mathsf{U}}_{1},{\mathsf{U}}_{2}\right\}
:

{\mathsf{U}}_{1}={\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\\
\hskip 11.74988pt{\mathsf{U}}_{2}={\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}(\mathtt{unit}).\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.

and these are the two SI trees such that
\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}=\left\{{\mathsf{V}}_{1},{\mathsf{V}}_{2}\right\}
:

{\mathsf{V}}_{1}={\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\hskip 11.74988pt{\mathsf{V}}_{2}={\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathtt{bool}).\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.

Therefore, for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, we can find

{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{U}}_{i}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
and

{\mathsf{W}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
such that

{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}
can be derived using
[
ref-
{\mathcal{B}}
]
.
For instance, since we have:

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathsf{U}}_{1}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}

\displaystyle=\big\{{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\,,\;{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathtt{bool}).\mathtt{end}\big\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}_{1}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}

\displaystyle=\big\{{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\,,\;{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}(\mathtt{unit}).\mathtt{end}\big\}

we can show that

{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\lesssim{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}
,
by the following coinductive derivation:
(we
highlight
the outputs matched by rule
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]
)

{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int})$}}.{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\lesssim{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int})$}}.\mathtt{end}{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\lesssim{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\mathtt{end}\lesssim\mathtt{end}

Hence, by
Definition

3.7
, we conclude that
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}
holds.

Example 3.10 (Example 3.21 by
Bravetti et al. 2019b
).

This example demonstrates how our subtyping, and its underlying
session decomposition approach, apply to a complex case, where the
subtyping proof requires infinite, non-cyclic derivations.
Consider the following session types:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbb{T}}=\mu\textbf{t}_{1}.\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{\ell_{1}}}}{({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).}{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}_{1}\\
{\mathit{{\ell_{2}}}}{({\mathsf{S}}_{2})}.\mu\textbf{t}_{2}.{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}_{2}\end{array}\right.\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}=\mu\textbf{t}_{1}.\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{\ell_{1}}}}{({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).}{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}_{1}\\
{\mathit{{\ell_{2}}}}{({\mathsf{S}}_{2})}.\mu\textbf{t}_{2}.{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}_{2}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\end{array}

We now prove that
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
.
Notably, if we omit the participant
{\sf p}
, we obtain binary session types
that are related under the
binary
asynchronous subtyping relation
by
Chen et al. 2017
— but due to its complexity, the relation cannot be proved using the binary asynchronous subtyping algorithm of
Bravetti et al. 2019a
;
Bravetti et al. 2019b
.

Letting
{\mathsf{T}}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)
and
{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)
,
by
Definition

3.7
we need to show that:

(1)

\forall{\mathsf{U}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\quad\forall{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\quad\exists{\mathsf{W}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{U}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\quad\exists{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\qquad{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}

Observe that both
{\mathsf{T}}
and
{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}
are SO trees. Therefore, we have

\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}=\{{\mathsf{T}}\}
;
moreover, all
{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
are SISO trees,
which means that, in (
1
),
for all such
{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}
we have

\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}=\{{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\}
. These singleton sets allow for simplifying the quantifications
in (
1
), as follows:

(2)

\forall{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\quad\exists{\mathsf{W}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\qquad{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}

and to prove
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,
it is enough to prove (
2
).
Before proceeding, we introduce
the following abbreviations (where
{\mathsf{W}}_{i}
are SISO trees,
and
\pi_{i}
are sequences of inputs and outputs, used to prefix a SISO tree):

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{W}}_{1}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}}\right)\qquad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}={\sf p}?\ell_{2}({\mathsf{S}}_{2}).\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}}\right)\\
{\mathsf{W}}_{3}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).\textbf{t}}\right)\\[-14.22636pt]
\pi_{1}={\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3})\qquad\pi_{3}={\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3})\qquad\pi_{i}^{n}=\overbrace{\pi_{i}.\dots.\pi_{i}}^{\text{\text{$n$ times}}}\end{array}

Then, we have:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}&=&\big\{{\mathsf{W}}_{3},\;{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\pi_{3}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\pi_{3}^{2}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\ldots,\;\pi_{3}^{n}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\dots\big\}\\
\llbracket{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}&=&\big\{{\mathsf{W}}_{1},\;{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\pi_{1}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\pi_{1}^{2}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\ldots,\;\pi_{1}^{n}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2},\;\dots\big\}\\
\end{array}

and we can prove (
2
) by showing that:

(i)

{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
and

(ii)

for all
n\geq 1
,
\pi_{3}^{n}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2}\lesssim\pi_{1}^{n}.{\mathsf{W}}_{2}

Here we develop item
(i)
.

(The complete proof of item
(ii)
and more details are given in Appendix
A.1
,
Example

A.9
)

The relation
{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
is proved by the following coinductive derivation:

{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}_{1}({\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}))^{3}.{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\mathsf{W}}_{1}({\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}))^{2}.{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}_{1}{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\mathsf{W}}_{1}{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\mathsf{W}}_{1}

where the topmost relation
holds by the following
infinite coinductive derivation,
for all
n\!\geq\!1
:

{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim({\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}))^{n}.{\mathsf{W}}_{1}({\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}))^{3}.{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim({\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}))^{n}.{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\mathsf{W}}_{1}({\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}))^{2}.{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim({\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}))^{n}.{\mathsf{W}}_{1}{\sf p}!\ell_{3}({\mathsf{S}}_{3}).{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim({\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}))^{n}.{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\mathsf{W}}_{1}{\mathsf{W}}_{3}\lesssim({\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}))^{n+1}.{\mathsf{W}}_{1}

4.
Typing System and Type Safety

Our multiparty session typing system blends the one by
Ghilezan et al. 2019
with the one in
(
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
, Section 7)
: like the latter, we type multiparty sessions without need for global types, thus simplifying our formalism and generalising our results. The key differences are our asynchronous subtyping (
Definition

3.7
) and our choice of
typing environment liveness
(
Definition

4.4
): their interplay yields our preciseness results.

4.1.
Typing System

Before proceeding, we need to formalise
queue types
for message queues, extending Def.
3.1
:

\sigma\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;\epsilon\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma\cdot\sigma

Type
\epsilon
denotes an empty queue;
{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}})
denotes a queued message with recipient
{\sf p}
, label
\ell
, and payload of sort
{\mathsf{S}}
; they are concatenated as
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.1 (Typing system).

The type system uses 4 judgments:

•

for expressions:
\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:{\mathsf{S}}

•

for queues:
\vdash h:\sigma

•

for processes:
\Theta\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}

•

for sessions:
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{M}

where the typing environments
\Gamma
and
\Theta
are defined as:

\Gamma\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;\emptyset\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Gamma,{\sf p}:(\sigma,{\mathbb{T}})\qquad\qquad\Theta\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;\emptyset\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Theta,X:{\mathbb{T}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{|\!\!|}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Theta,x:{\mathsf{S}}

The typing system is inductively defined by the rules in
Table

5
.

The judgment for expressions is standard:
\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:{\mathsf{S}}
means that,
given the variables and sorts in environment
\Theta
,
expression
\mathsf{e}
is of the sort
{\mathsf{S}}
.
The judgment for queues means that queue
h
has queue type
\sigma
.
The judgment for processes states that,
given the types of the variables in
\Theta
,
process
P
behaves as prescribed by
{\mathbb{T}}
.
The judgment for sessions states that multiple participants and queues behave as prescribed by
\Gamma
, which maps each participant
{\sf p}
to the pairing of a queue type (for
{\sf p}
’s message queue) and a session type (for
{\sf p}
’s process).
If
\Theta=\emptyset
we write
\vdash\mathsf{e}:{\mathsf{S}}
and
\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}.

\begin{array}[]{c}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{n}:\mathtt{nat}\qquad{\Theta\vdash(-\mathsf{n}):\mathtt{int}\qquad\Theta\vdash\texttt{0}:\mathtt{int}}\quad\Theta\vdash\mathsf{true}:\mathtt{bool}\quad\Theta\vdash\mathsf{false}:\mathtt{bool}\\
{\Theta\vdash():\mathtt{unit}}\quad\Theta,x:{\mathsf{S}}\vdash x:{\mathsf{S}}\quad\Theta\vdash{\sf succ}(\mathsf{e}):\mathtt{nat}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{nat}\quad\Theta\vdash{\sf inv}(\mathsf{e}):\mathtt{int}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{int}\\[5.69054pt]
\Theta\vdash\neg\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}{}\qquad{\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}>\texttt{0}:\mathtt{bool}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{int}}\qquad{\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}\approx():\mathtt{bool}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{unit}}\qquad\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:{\mathsf{S}}\quad{\mathsf{S}}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\\[5.69054pt]
\vdash\varnothing:\epsilon\qquad\vdash({\sf q},\ell(\mathsf{v})):{\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}})\ \vdash\mathsf{v}:{\mathsf{S}}\qquad\vdash h_{1}\cdot h_{2}:\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{2}\vdash h_{1}:\sigma_{1}\quad\vdash h_{2}:\sigma_{2}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\vdash\mathbf{0}:\mathtt{end}$\;$\quad\;\Theta,X:{\mathbb{T}}\vdash X:{\mathbb{T}}$\;$\quad\;\Theta\vdash\mu X.P:{\mathbb{T}}\Theta,X:{\mathbb{T}}\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}\quad\;\Theta\vdash{\sf q}!\ell\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P:{\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathbb{T}}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:{\mathsf{S}}\quad\Theta\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}\\[8.53581pt]
\Theta\vdash\sum_{i\in I}{\sf q}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).P_{i}:\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf q}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{{\mathbb{T}}_{i}}\forall i\in I\;\;\;\Theta,x_{i}:{\mathsf{S}}_{i}\vdash P_{i}:{\mathbb{T}}_{i}\hskip 23.49976pt\Theta\vdash\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \mathsf{e}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ P_{1}\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ P_{2}:{\mathbb{T}}\Theta\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}\hskip 11.74988pt\Theta\vdash P_{i}:{\mathbb{T}}\ \text{\tiny$(i=1,2)$}\\[8.53581pt]
\Theta\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\Theta\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}\quad{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\qquad\Gamma\vdash\prod_{i\in I}({\sf p}_{i}\triangleleft P_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}_{i}\triangleleft h_{i})\Gamma=\{{\sf p}_{i}{:}\,(\sigma_{i},{\mathbb{T}}_{i})\;|\;i\in I\}\quad\forall i\in I\;\;\vdash P_{i}:{\mathbb{T}}_{i}\;\;\vdash h_{i}:\sigma_{i}\end{array}

Table 5
.
Typing rules for expressions and queues (top 3 rows),
and for processes and sessions (bottom 4 rows).

We now comment the rules for processes and sessions (other rules are self-explanatory).
Rule
[
t-
\mathbf{0}
]
types a terminated process.
Rule
[
t-var
]
types a process variable with the assumption in the environment. By
[
t-rec
]
, a recursive process is typed with
{\mathbb{T}}
if the process variable
X
and the body
P
have the same type
{\mathbb{T}}
.
By
[
t-out
]
, an output process is typed with a singleton selection type, if the message being sent is of the correct sort, and the process continuation has the continuation type.
By
[
t-ext
]
, a process external choice is typed as a branching type
with matching participant
{\sf p}
and labels
\ell_{i}
(for all
i\!\in\!I
); in the rule premise, each continuation process
P_{i}
must be typed with the corresponding continuation type
{\mathbb{T}}_{i}
, assuming the bound variable
x_{i}
is of sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{i}
(for all
i\!\in\!I
). By
[
t-cond
]
, a conditional has type
{\mathbb{T}}
if its expression has sort
\mathtt{bool}
, and its “
\mathsf{then}
” and “
\mathsf{else}
” branches have type
{\mathbb{T}}
.
Rule
[
t-sub
]
is the
subsumption rule
: it states that a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}
is also typed by any supertype of
{\mathbb{T}}
(and since
\leqslant
relates types up-to unfolding, this rule makes our type system
equi-recursive

(
Pierce 2002
)
). By
[
t-sess
]
, a session
\mathcal{M}
is typed by environment
\Gamma
if all the participants in
\mathcal{M}
have processes and queues typed by the session and queue types pairs in
\Gamma
.

Example 4.2.

The processes
P_{\sf r}
and its optimised version
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}

in Section
1
are typable using the rules in Def.
4.1
,
and the types
{\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
in Example
3.9
. In particular,
P_{\sf r}
has type
{\mathbb{T}}
,
while
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
has type
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, since
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}
, by rule
[
t-sub
]
the optimised process
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
has
also
type
{\mathbb{T}}
:
hence, our type system allows using
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}

whenever a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}
(such as
P_{\sf r}
) is expected.

4.2.
Typing Environment Reductions and Liveness

To formulate the soundness result for our type system,
we define typing environment reductions.
The reductions rely on a standard structural congruence relation
\equiv
over queue types,
allowing to reorder messages with different recipients.
Formally,
\equiv
is the least congruence satisfying:

\begin{array}[]{c}\sigma\cdot\epsilon\;\equiv\;\epsilon\cdot\sigma\;\equiv\;\sigma\qquad\qquad\sigma_{1}\cdot(\sigma_{2}\cdot\sigma_{3})\;\equiv\;(\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{2})\cdot\sigma_{3}\\[2.84526pt]
{\sigma\cdot{\sf p}_{1}!\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1})\cdot{\sf p}_{2}!\ell_{2}({\mathsf{S}}_{2})\cdot\sigma^{\prime}\;\;\equiv\;\;\sigma\cdot{\sf p}_{2}!\ell_{2}({\mathsf{S}}_{2})\cdot{\sf p}_{1}!\ell_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1})\cdot\sigma^{\prime}}\quad\text{(if ${\sf p}_{1}\neq{\sf p}_{2}$)}\end{array}

For pairs of queue/session types,
we define
structural congruence as
(\sigma_{1},{\mathbb{T}}_{1})\equiv(\sigma_{2},{\mathbb{T}}_{2})

iff
\sigma_{1}\equiv\sigma_{2}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}\equiv{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
,   and
subtyping
as
(\sigma_{1},{\mathbb{T}}_{1})\leqslant(\sigma_{2},{\mathbb{T}}_{2})
iff

\sigma_{1}\equiv\sigma_{2}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{1}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
. We extend subtyping and congruence to typing environments,
by requiring all corresponding entries to be related,
and allowing additional unrelated entries of type
(\epsilon,\mathtt{end})
.
More formally, we write
\Gamma\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}
(resp.
\Gamma\leqslant\Gamma^{\prime}
)  iff
\forall{\sf p}\!\in\!dom(\Gamma)\cap dom(\Gamma^{\prime}){:}

\Gamma({\sf p})\!\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}({\sf p})
(resp.
\Gamma({\sf p})\leqslant\Gamma^{\prime}({\sf p})
) and
\forall{\sf p}\!\in\!dom(\Gamma)\setminus dom(\Gamma^{\prime}),{\sf q}\!\in\!dom(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus dom(\Gamma){:}

\Gamma({\sf p})\!\equiv\!(\epsilon,\mathtt{end})\!\equiv\!\Gamma^{\prime}({\sf q})
.

Definition 4.3 (Typing environment reduction).

The reduction
\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}
of asynchronous session typing environments is inductively defined as follows,
with
\alpha
being either

{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}?{\ell}

or
{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}!{\ell}

(for some
{\sf p},{\sf q},\ell
):

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{\qquad}r@{}}\text{\scriptsize[{e-rcv}]}&\hskip-8.53581pt{{\sf p}{:}\,({\sf q}!\ell_{k}({\mathsf{S}}_{k}^{\prime}){\cdot}\sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{{\sf p}}),{\sf q}{:}\,(\sigma_{{\sf q}},\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{{\mathbb{T}}_{i}}),\Gamma\;\xrightarrow{{{\sf q}}{:}{{\sf p}}?{\ell_{k}}}\;{\sf p}{:}\,(\sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf p}),{\sf q}{:}\,(\sigma_{{\sf q}},{\mathbb{T}}_{k}),\Gamma}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\hskip 0.0pt(k\!\in\!I,{\mathsf{S}}_{k}^{\prime}\!\leq\vcentcolon\!{\mathsf{S}}_{k})\\
\text{\scriptsize[{e-send}]}&{{\sf p}:(\sigma,\tinternal_{i\in I}{\sf q}!\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{{\mathbb{T}}_{i}}),\Gamma\;\;\xrightarrow{{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}!{\ell_{k}}}\;\;{\sf p}:(\sigma{\cdot}{\sf q}!\ell_{k}({\mathsf{S}}_{k}){\cdot}\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}_{k}),\Gamma}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&{(k\in I)}\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\scriptsize[{e-struct}]}&\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\equiv\Gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}\;\implies\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}\Gamma^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\end{array}

We often write
\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
instead of
\Gamma\xrightarrow{\;\alpha\;}\Gamma^{\prime}
when
\alpha
is not important.
We write
\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{\!*}}
for the reflexive and transitive closure of
\longrightarrow
.

Rule
[
e-rcv
]
says that an environment can take a reduction step if participant
{\sf p}
has a message toward
{\sf q}
with label
\ell_{k}
and payload sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
at the head of its queue, while
{\sf q}
’s type is a branching from
{\sf p}

including label
\ell_{k}
and a corresponding sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{k}

supertype of
{\mathsf{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
; the environment evolves with a reduction labelled

{{\sf q}}{:}{{\sf p}}{?}{\ell_{k}}
, by consuming
{\sf p}
’s message and activating the continuation
{\mathbb{T}}_{k}
in
{\sf q}
’s type. In rule
[
e-send
]
the environment evolves by letting participant
{\sf p}
(having a selection type) send a message toward
{\sf q}
; the reduction is labelled
{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}{!}{\ell_{k}}
(with
\ell_{k}
being a selection label), and places the message at the end of
{\sf p}
’s queue. Rule
[
e-struct
]
closes the reduction under structural congruence.

Similarly to
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
, we define a behavioral property of typing environments (and their reductions) called
liveness
:
2
2

2

Notably, our definition of liveness is stronger than the “liveness” in
(
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
, Fig. 5)
, and is closer to “liveness
+
” therein:
we adopt it because a weaker “liveness” would not allow to achieve Theorem
7.3
later on.

we will use it as a precondition for typing, to ensure that typed processes cannot reduce to any error in Table
4
.

Definition 4.4 (Live typing environment).

A
typing environment path
is possibly infinite sequence of typing environments
(\Gamma_{i})_{i\in I}
, where
I=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
is a set of consecutive natural numbers, and,
\forall i\in I
,
\Gamma_{i}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{i+1}
. We say that a path
(\Gamma_{i})_{i\in I}
is
fair
iff,
\forall i\in I
:

(F1)

whenever
\Gamma_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}!{\ell}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, then
\exists k,\ell^{\prime}
such that
I\ni k{+}1>i
, and
\Gamma_{k}\xrightarrow{{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}!{\ell^{\prime}}}\Gamma_{k+1}

(F2)

whenever
\Gamma_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}?{\ell}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, then
\exists k
such that
I\ni k{+}1>i
, and
\Gamma_{k}\xrightarrow{{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}?{\ell}}\Gamma_{k+1}

We say that a path
(\Gamma_{i})_{i\in I}
is
live
iff,
\forall i\in I
:

(L1)

if
\Gamma_{i}({\sf p})\equiv\left({\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}})\cdot\sigma\,,\,{\mathbb{T}}\right)
, then
\exists k
:
I\ni k{+}1>i
and
\Gamma_{k}\xrightarrow{{{\sf q}}{:}{{\sf p}}?{\ell}}\Gamma_{k+1}

(L2)

if
\Gamma_{i}({\sf p})\equiv\left(\sigma_{\sf p}\,,\,\texternal_{j\in J}{{\sf q}?\ell_{j}({\mathsf{S}}_{j}).{{\mathbb{T}}_{j}}}\right)
, then
\exists k,\ell^{\prime}
:
I\ni k{+}1>i
and
\Gamma_{k}\xrightarrow{{{\sf p}}{:}{{\sf q}}?{\ell^{\prime}}}\Gamma_{k+1}

We say that a typing environment
\Gamma
is
live
iff all fair paths beginning with
\Gamma
are live.

By Def.
4.4
, a path is a (possibly infinite) sequence of reductions of a typing environment. Intuitively, a fair path represents a “fair scheduling:” along its reductions, every pending internal choice eventually enqueues a message (
F1
), and every pending message reception is eventually performed (
F2
). A path is live if, along its reductions, every queued message is eventually consumed (
L1
), and every waiting external choice eventually consumes a queued message (
L2
). A typing environment is live if it always yields a live path when it is fairly scheduled.

Example 4.5 (Fairness and liveness).

Consider the typing environment:

\Gamma\;=\;{\sf p}{:}(\epsilon,\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}),\,{\sf q}{:}(\epsilon,\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t})

The typing environment
\Gamma
has an infinite path where
{\sf p}
keeps
enqueuing outputs, while
{\sf q}
never fires a reduction to receive them:
such a path is unfair, because
{\sf q}
’s message reception is always enabled,
but never performed. Instead, in all fair paths of
\Gamma
, the messages enqueued by
{\sf p}

are eventually consumed by
{\sf q}
, and the inputs of
{\sf q}

are eventually fired: hence,
\Gamma
is live.
Now consider:

\textstyle\Gamma^{\prime}\;=\;{\sf p}{:}\left(\epsilon,\mu\textbf{t}.\tinternal\!\big\{{\sf q}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t},\,{\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}).{\sf r}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\big\}\right),\,{\sf q}{:}\left(\epsilon,\mu\textbf{t}.\texternal\!\big\{{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\,,{\sf p}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\big\}\right),\,{\sf r}{:}\left(\epsilon,\mu\textbf{t}.{\sf p}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\right)

The environment
\Gamma^{\prime}
has fair and live paths
where
{\sf p}
chooses to send
\ell^{\prime}
to
{\sf q}
and then to
{\sf r}
.
However, there is also a fair path where
{\sf p}

always chooses to send
\ell
to
{\sf q}
:
in this case,
{\sf q}
always eventually receives a message,
but
{\sf r}
