forever waits for an input that will never arrive.
Therefore, such a path is fair but not live, hence
\Gamma^{\prime}
is not live.

\blacksquare

Liveness is preserved by environment reductions and
subtyping, as formalised in
Proposition

4.6

and
Lemma

4.7
:
these properties are crucial for
proving subject reduction later on.

(proofs in
Section

B.1
)

Proposition 4.6.

If
\Gamma
is live and
\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is live.

Lemma 4.7.

If
\Gamma
is live and
\Gamma^{\prime}\leqslant\Gamma
, then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is live.

We can now state our subject reduction result
(
Theorem

4.8
below): if session
\mathcal{M}
is typed by a live
\Gamma
, and
\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{M}
might anticipate some inputs/outputs prescribed by
\Gamma
, as allowed by subtyping
\leqslant
. Hence,
\mathcal{M}
reduces by following some
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\!\leqslant\!\Gamma
, which evolves to
\Gamma^{\prime}
that types
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

(Proofs:
Section

B.2
.)

Theorem 4.8 (Subject Reduction).

Assume
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{M}
with
\Gamma
live. If
\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,  then there are live type environments
\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\Gamma
,
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Corollary 4.9 (Type Safety and Progress).

Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{M}
with
\Gamma
live. Then,
\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow^{*}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
implies
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{error}
;  also, either
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\equiv{\sf p}\triangleleft\mathbf{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft\varnothing
,  or
\exists\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\neq\mathsf{error}
.

Notably, since our errors (Table
4
) include orphan messages, deadlocks, and starvation, Corollary
4.9
implies
session liveness
: a typed session will never deadlock, all its external choices will be eventually activated, all its queued messages will be eventually consumed.
3
3

3

This assumes that a session is fairly scheduled,
similarly to the notion of “fair path” in
Definition

4.4
.

5.
Preciseness of Asynchronous Multiparty Session Subtyping

We now present our main result.
A subtyping relation
\leqslant
is
sound
if it satisfies the
Liskov and Wing 1994
substitution principle: if
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, then a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
engaged in a well-typed session may be safely replaced with a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}
. The reversed implication is called
completeness
: if it is always safe to replace
a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
with a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}
,
then we should have
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
. If a subtyping
\leqslant
is both sound and complete,
then
\leqslant
is
precise
. This is formalised in
Definition

5.1
below (where we use the contrapositive of the completeness implication).

Definition 5.1 (Preciseness).

Let
\unlhd
be a preorder over session types. We say that
\unlhd
is:

(1)

a
sound subtyping
if
{\mathbb{T}}\unlhd{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
implies that, for all
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}),\mathcal{M},P
, the following holds:

(a)

if
\big(\forall Q:\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\;\implies\;\;\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}
for some live
\Gamma\big)
then

\big(\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}\;\implies\;({\sf r}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\longrightarrow^{*}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
implies
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{error})\big)

(2)

a
complete subtyping
if
{\mathbb{T}}\mathrel{\not\!\!\unlhd}{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
implies that there are
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}),\mathcal{M},P
such that:

(a)

\forall Q:\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\;\implies\;\;\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}
for some live
\Gamma

(b)

\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}

(c)

{\sf r}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\longrightarrow^{*}\mathsf{error}
.

(3)

a
precise subtyping
if it is both sound and complete.

As customary, our subtyping relation is embedded in the type system via a subsumption rule, giving soundness as an immediate consequence of the subject reduction property.

Theorem 5.2 (Soundness).

The asynchronous multiparty session subtyping
\leqslant
is sound.

Proof.

Take any
{\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, and
{\sf r},\mathcal{M}
satisfying the following condition:

(3)

\displaystyle\forall Q:\;\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\;\implies\;\;\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\;\text{ for some live $\Gamma$}

If
\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}
, we derive by
[
t-sub
]
that
\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
holds. By (
3
),
\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}
for some live
\Gamma.

Hence, by Corollary
4.9
,
{\sf r}\triangleleft P\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}\longrightarrow^{*}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
implies
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{error}
.
∎

The proof of completeness of
\leqslant
is much more involved.
We show that
\leqslant
satisfies item
(2)
of
Definition

5.1
in 4 steps, that we develop in the next sections:

[Step 1]:

We define the
negation
\not\lesssim
of the SISO trees refinement relation
by an inductive definition, thus getting a clear characterisation of the complement
\not\leqslant
of the subtyping relation, that is necessary for Step 2. In addition, for every pair
{\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, we choose a pair
{\mathbb{U}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
satisfying
{\mathbb{U}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
and

\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}.

[Step 2]:

We define for every
{\mathbb{U}}
a
characteristic process

{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
. Moreover, we prove that if
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}
then we have
\vdash{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right):{\mathbb{T}}
.

[Step 3]:

For every
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
with
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}},
and for every participant
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}),
we define a
characteristic session

\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
, which is typable if composed with a process
Q
of type
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
:

\forall Q:\;\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\;\implies\;\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\;\text{ for some live $\Gamma$}.

[Step 4]:

Finally, we show that for all

{\mathbb{U}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathbb{U}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
, the characteristic session
\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
(Step 3) reduces to error if composed with the characteristic process of
{\mathbb{U}}
(Step 2):

{\sf r}\triangleleft{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\;\longrightarrow^{*}\;\mathsf{error}.

Hence, we prove the completeness of
\leqslant
by showing that, for all
{\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, we can find
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{T}}^{\prime})
,
P={\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
(Steps 1,2), and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
(Step 3) satisfying Def.
5.1
(2)
(Step 4). We now illustrate each step in more detail.

5.1.
Step 1: Subtyping Negation

{\small\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\sf p}!\not\in\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})\;\;{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\sf p}?\not\in\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\sf p}!\not\in\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\sf p}?\not\in\mathtt{act}({\mathsf{W}})\\[4.2679pt]
{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\not\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}\;\;{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\\[4.2679pt]
{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}?\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\not\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}\\[4.2679pt]
{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$\ $\;\;{\sf p}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{({\sf p})}.{\sf q}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$\ $\\[4.2679pt]
{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}\not\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}{\mathsf{S}}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\\[4.2679pt]
{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}!\ell^{\prime}({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{S}}\not\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\;\;{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{{\sf p}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}).{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{S}}\leq\vcentcolon{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim\mathcal{B}^{({\sf p})}.{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\end{array}}

Table 6
.
The relation
\not\lesssim
between SISO trees.

In Table
6
we
inductively

define the relation
\not\lesssim
over SISO trees: it contains all pairs of SISO trees that are
not
related by
\lesssim
,
as stated in Lemma
5.3
below. This step is necessary because, in Step 2 (
Section

5.2
),
we will need the shape of the types related by
\not\lesssim

in order to generate some corresponding processes.

The first category of rules checks whether
two SISO trees have a direct syntactic mismatch:
whether their sets of actions are disjoint (
\text{\scriptsize[{n-out}]},

\text{\scriptsize[{n-inp}]},

\text{\scriptsize[{n-out-R}]},

\text{\scriptsize[{n-inp-R}]})
;
the label of the LHS is
not equal to the label of the RHS (
[
n-inp-
\ell
]
,
[
n-out-
\ell
]
);
or
matching labels are followed by mismatching sorts or continuations (
[
n-inp-
{\mathsf{S}}
]
,
\text{\scriptsize[{n-out-${\mathsf{S}}$}]},

[
n-inp-
{\mathsf{W}}
]
,
[
n-out-
{\mathsf{W}}
]
).

The second category checks more subtle cases related to
asynchronous permutations;
rule
[
n-
\mathcal{A}
-
\ell
]
checks a label mismatch
when the input on the RHS is preceded by a finite number of inputs from other participant; similarly,
rules
[
n-
\mathcal{A}
-
{\mathsf{S}}
]
and
[
n-
\mathcal{A}
-
{\mathsf{W}}
]

check mismatching sorts or continuations.
Rules
[
n-i-o-1
]
and
[
n-i-o-2
]

formulate the cases such that
the top prefix on the LHS is input and the top sequence of prefixes on
the RHS consists of a finite number of inputs from other participants
and/or outputs.
Finally, rules
[
N-
\mathcal{B}
-
\ell
]
,

[
n-
\mathcal{B}
-
{\mathsf{S}}
]
and
[
n-
\mathcal{B}
-
{\mathsf{W}}
]

check the cases of label mismatch, or
matching labels followed by mismatching sorts, or continuations
of the two types with output prefixes targeting a same participant, where the RHS is prefixed by a finite number of outputs (to other participants) and/or inputs (to any participant).

More in detail, according to the rules in
Table

6
,
two types are related by
\not\lesssim
if:

•

their sets of actions are disjunctive (
\text{\scriptsize[{n-out}]},

\text{\scriptsize[{n-inp}]},

\text{\scriptsize[{n-out-R}]},

\text{\scriptsize[{n-inp-R}]})
;

•

their top prefixes are both inputs or both outputs, targeting the same participant — but the label of the LHS is
not equal to the label of the RHS (
[
n-inp-
\ell
]
,
[
n-out-
\ell
]
);

•

their top prefixes are both inputs or outputs, targeting the same participant, with matching labels followed by mismatching sorts or mismatching continuations (
[
n-inp-
{\mathsf{S}}
]
,
\text{\scriptsize[{n-out-${\mathsf{S}}$}]},

[
n-inp-
{\mathsf{W}}
]
,
[
n-out-
{\mathsf{W}}
]
);

•

they both have inputs from a same participant
{\sf p}
, and such input on the RHS is preceded by a finite number of inputs from other participants — but the input label from
{\sf p}
on the LHS is not equal to the label on the RHS (
[
n-
\mathcal{A}
-
\ell
]
);

•

they both have inputs from a same participant
{\sf p}
, and such input on the RHS is preceded by a finite number of inputs from other participants — but the inputs from
{\sf p}
have matching labels followed by mismatching sorts or mismatching continuations (
[
n-
\mathcal{A}
-
{\mathsf{S}}
]
,
[
n-
\mathcal{A}
-
{\mathsf{W}}
]
);

•

the top prefix on the LHS is an input from a participant
{\sf p}
, but the top-level prefixes on the RHS consists of a finite number of inputs (
0
or more) from other participants, followed by an output (
[
n-i-o-1
]
,
[
n-i-o-2
]
);

•

they both have outputs toward a same participant
{\sf p}
, and the output on the RHS is preceded by a finite number of outputs to other participants and/or inputs — but the output label towards
{\sf p}
on the LHS is
not equal to the label on the RHS (
[
N-
\mathcal{B}
-
\ell
]
);

•

they both have outputs toward a same participant
{\sf p}
, and the output on the RHS is preceded by a finite number of outputs to other participants and/or inputs — but the outputs toward
{\sf p}
have matching labels followed by mismatching sorts or mismatching continuations (
[
n-
\mathcal{B}
-
{\mathsf{S}}
]
,
[
n-
\mathcal{B}
-
{\mathsf{W}}
]
).

Lemma 5.3.

Take any pair of SISO trees
{\mathsf{W}}
and
{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
. Then,
{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}

is
not
derivable
if and only if

{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
is derivable with the rules in
Table

6
.

Proof.

We adopt an approach inspired by
Blackburn et al. 2012
and
Ligatti
et al. 2017
.

For the “
\Rightarrow
” direction of the statement,
assume that
{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
is
not
derivable:
this means that, if we attempt to build a derivation
by applying the rules in
Definition

3.2
,
starting with “
{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
” and moving upwards,
we obtain a
failing derivation

that, after a finite number
n
of rule applications,
reaches a (wrong) judgement “
{\mathsf{W}}_{n}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}_{n}
”
on which no rule of
Definition

3.2
can be further applied.
Then, by induction on
n
, we transform such a failing derivation
into an
actual
derivation based on the rules for
\not\lesssim

in
Table

6
, which proves
{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
.

For the “
\Leftarrow
” direction,
assume that
{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
holds, by the rules in
Table

6
:
from its derivation, we construct a failing derivation
that starts with “
{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
”
and is based on the rules of
Definition

3.2

— which implies that
{\mathsf{W}}\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
is
not
derivable.

(Full proof in
Section

C.1
)

∎

It is immediate from
Definition

3.7
and
Lemma

5.3

that
{\mathbb{T}}
is
not
a subtype of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, written
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, if and only if:

(4)

\exists{\mathsf{U}}\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\;\;\exists{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\;\;\forall{\mathsf{W}}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{U}}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\;\;\forall{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}

Moreover, we prove that whenever
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, we can find
regular, syntax-derived
SO/SI trees usable as the witnesses
{\mathsf{U}},{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}
in (
4
).

(See Appendix, page
C.2
.)
Thus, from this result and by (
4
),
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}

implies:

(5)

\exists{\mathbb{U}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}:\;\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\;\;\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\;\;\forall{\mathsf{W}}\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}\;\;\forall{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}\;\;{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}

Example 5.4.

Consider the example in Section
1
, and its types
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbb{T}}
in Example
3.9
:

\small{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}=\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.\hskip 10.80002pt{\mathbb{T}}=\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{success}}}{(\mathtt{int}).}\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\\
{\mathit{{error}}}{(\mathtt{bool})}.\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\mathit{{cont}}}{(\mathtt{int})}.\mathtt{end}\\
{\mathit{{stop}}}{(\mathtt{unit})}.\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

We have seen that
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}
holds (Example
3.9
), and thus, by subsumption, our type system allows to use the optimised process
P^{\prime}_{\sf r}
in place of
P_{\sf r}
(Example
4.2
).
We now show that the inverse relation does
not
hold, i.e.,
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, hence the inverse process replacement is disallowed.
Take, e.g.,
{\mathbb{U}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
as follows, noticing that
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
:

\small{\mathbb{U}}=\texternal{\sf p}?\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\\
\mathit{{error}}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}(\mathtt{unit}).\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.\hskip 21.60004pt{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}=\tinternal{\sf q}!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\\
\mathit{{stop}}(\mathtt{unit}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathtt{bool}).\mathtt{end}\end{array}\right.

For all
{\mathsf{W}}\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}=\left\{{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\,,\,{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}(\mathtt{unit}).\mathtt{end}\right\}
and all
{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}=\left\{{\sf q}!\mathit{{cont}}(\mathtt{int}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{success}}(\mathtt{int}).\mathtt{end}\,,\,{\sf q}!\mathit{{stop}}(\mathtt{unit}).{\sf p}?\mathit{{error}}(\mathtt{bool}).\mathtt{end}\right\}
we get by
[
n-i-o-1
]
that

{\mathsf{W}}\not\lesssim{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
. Therefore, we conclude
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
.

5.2.
Step 2: Characteristic Processes

For any SO type
{\mathbb{U}}
, we define a characteristic process
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
(Def.
5.5
): intuitively, it is a process constructed to communicate as prescribed by
{\mathbb{U}}
, and to be typable by
{\mathbb{U}}
.

Definition 5.5.

The characteristic process
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
of type
{\mathbb{U}}
is defined inductively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{P}}\!\left(\mathtt{end}\right)=\mathbf{0}\qquad{\mathcal{P}}\!\left(\textbf{t}\right)=X_{\textbf{t}}\qquad{\mathcal{P}}\!\left(\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{U}}\right)=\mu X_{\textbf{t}}.{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)\qquad{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\sf p}!{\ell}({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathbb{U}}\right)={\sf p}!\ell\langle\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{val$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsf{S}}}\rrparenthesis$}}}\rangle.{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)\\
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left(\texternal_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{i}).{\mathbb{U}}_{i}\right)\;=\;\sum_{i\in I}{\sf p}?\ell_{i}(x_{i}).\mathsf{if}\penalty\ \text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{expr$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsf{S}}_{i}}\rrparenthesis$}}}\penalty\ \mathsf{then}\penalty\ {\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}_{i}\right)\penalty\ \mathsf{else}\penalty\ {\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}_{i}\right)\\[2.84526pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\text{where: }\\[2.84526pt]
\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{val$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{nat}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=1\quad\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{val$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{int}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=-1\quad\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{val$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{bool}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=\mathsf{true}\quad{\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{val$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{unit}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=()\quad\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{expr$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{unit}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=(x\approx())}\\
\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{expr$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{bool}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=(\neg x)\quad\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{expr$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{nat}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=({\sf succ}(x)>\texttt{0})\quad\text{\rm{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@text@underline{expr$\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{int}}\rrparenthesis$}}}=({\sf inv}(x)>\texttt{0})\end{array}\end{array}

By
Definition

5.5
, for every output in
{\mathbb{U}}
, the characteristic
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
sends a value
val
\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsf{S}}}\rrparenthesis
of the expected sort
{\mathsf{S}}
; and for every external choice in
{\mathbb{U}}
,
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
performs a branching, and uses any received value
x_{i}
of sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{i}

in a boolean expression
expr
\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsf{S}}_{i}}\rrparenthesis
:
the expression will cause an
\mathsf{error}
if the value of
x_{i}

is not of sort
{\mathsf{S}}_{i}
.

Crucially, for all
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{U}}
such that
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
(e.g., from (
5
) above), we have
{\mathbb{U}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}
: therefore,
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
is also typable by
{\mathbb{T}}
, as per
Proposition

5.6
below.
(Proof:
Appendix

C
)

Proposition 5.6.

For all closed types
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{U}}
, if
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
then
\vdash{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right):{\mathbb{T}}
.

5.3.
Step 3: Characteristic Session

The next step to prove completeness is to define for each session type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
and participant
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
a
characteristic session

\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},\mathbb{V^{\prime}}}
, that is well typed (with a live typing environment) when composed with participant
{\sf r}
associated with a process of type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
and empty queue.

For a SI type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
and
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})=\{{\sf p}_{1},\ldots,{\sf p}_{m}\}
, we define
m

characteristic SO session types
where participants
{\sf p}_{1},\ldots,{\sf p}_{m}
are engaged in a live multiparty interaction with
{\sf r}
, and with each other.
Definition

5.7
ensures that after each communication between
{\sf r}
and some
{\sf p}\!\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
, there is a cyclic sequence of communications starting with
{\sf p}
, involving
all
other
{\sf q}\!\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
, and ending with
{\sf p}
— with each participant acting both as receiver, and as sender.

Definition 5.7.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
be a SI session type and
{\sf r}\!\not\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})\!=\!\{{\sf p}_{1},{...},{\sf p}_{m}\}
. For every
k\!\in\!\{1,{...},m\}
, if
m\!\geq\!2
we define a
characteristic SO session type

{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})
as follows:

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}{\tt cyclic}(\mathtt{end},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&\mathtt{end}\\
{\tt cyclic}(\textbf{t},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&\textbf{t}\\
{\tt cyclic}(\mu\textbf{t}.{\mathbb{V}}_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&\mu\textbf{t}.{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})\\
{\tt cyclic}({\sf p}_{k}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&{\sf r}!\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf p}_{k+1}!\ell(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}_{k-1}?\ell(\mathtt{bool}).$}}{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})\\
{\tt cyclic}({\sf q}?\ell({\mathsf{S}}).{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&{\sf p}_{k-1}?\ell(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}_{k+1}!\ell(\mathtt{bool}).{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})\;\;\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}(if ${\sf q}\!\neq\!{\sf p}_{k}$)}}\\
{\tt cyclic}(\tinternal_{j\in J}{\sf p}_{k}!\ell_{j}({\mathsf{S}}_{j}).{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}_{j},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&\texternal_{j\in J}{\sf r}?\ell_{j}({\mathsf{S}}_{j}).{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}${\sf p}_{k+1}!\ell_{j}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}_{k-1}?\ell_{j}(\mathtt{bool}).$}}{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}_{j},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})\\
{\tt cyclic}(\tinternal_{j\in J}{\sf q}!\ell_{j}({\mathsf{S}}_{j}).{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}_{j},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})&=&\texternal_{j\in J}{\sf p}_{k-1}?\ell_{j}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}_{k+1}!\ell_{j}(\mathtt{bool}).{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}_{j},{\sf p}_{k},{\sf r})\quad{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}(if ${\sf q}\!\neq\!{\sf p}_{k}$)}}\end{array}

If
m\!=\!1
(i.e., if there is only one participant in
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
) we define
{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p}_{1},{\sf r})
as above, but we omit the
(highlighted) cyclic communications
, and the cases with
{\sf q}\!\neq\!{\sf p}_{k}
do not apply.

Example 5.8 (Characteristic session types).

Consider the following SI type:

{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}\;=\;\mu\textbf{t}.\tinternal{}{\big\{{\sf q}!\ell_{2}(\mathtt{nat}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}(\mathtt{nat}).\textbf{t}\,,\,{\sf q}!\ell_{3}(\mathtt{nat}).{\sf p}?\ell_{4}(\mathtt{nat}).\textbf{t}\big\}}

Let
{\sf r}\!\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
. The characteristic session types for participants
{\sf p},{\sf q}\!\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
are:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\,}c@{\,\;}l@{}}{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p},{\sf r})&=&\mu\textbf{t}.\texternal{}{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf q}?\ell_{2}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf q}!\ell_{2}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf r}!\ell_{1}(\mathtt{nat}).{\sf q}!\ell_{1}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf q}?\ell_{1}(\mathtt{bool}).\textbf{t}\\
{\sf q}?\ell_{3}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf q}!\ell_{3}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf r}!\ell_{4}(\mathtt{nat}).{\sf q}!\ell_{4}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf q}?\ell_{4}(\mathtt{bool}).\textbf{t}\end{array}\right.}\\[8.53581pt]
{\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf q},{\sf r})&=&\mu\textbf{t}.\texternal{}{\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\sf r}?\ell_{2}(\mathtt{nat}).{\sf p}!\ell_{2}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}?\ell_{2}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}?\ell_{1}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}!\ell_{1}(\mathtt{bool}).\textbf{t}\\
{\sf r}?\ell_{3}(\mathtt{nat}).{\sf p}!\ell_{3}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}?\ell_{3}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}?\ell_{4}(\mathtt{bool}).{\sf p}!\ell_{4}(\mathtt{bool}).\textbf{t}\end{array}\right.}\end{array}

Note that if
{\sf r}
follows type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
, then it must select and send to
{\sf q}
one message between
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{3}
; correspondingly, the characteristic session type for
{\sf q}
receives the message (with a branching), and propagates it to
{\sf p}
, who sends it back to
{\sf q}
(cyclic communication). Then,
{\sf r}
waits for a message from
{\sf p}
(either
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{4}
, depending on the previous selection): correspondingly, the characteristic session type for

{\sf p}
will send such a message, and also propagate it to
{\sf q}
with a cyclic communication.
\blacksquare

Given an SI type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
, we can use Def.
5.7
to construct the following typing environment:

(6)

\displaystyle\Gamma\,=\,\{{\sf r}{:}\,(\epsilon,{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})\}\cup\left\{{\sf p}{:}\,(\epsilon,{\mathbb{U}}_{{\sf p}})\;\middle|\;{\sf p}\!\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})\right\}\quad\text{where\, $\forall{\sf p}\!\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}){:}\,{\mathbb{U}}_{{\sf p}}={\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p},{\sf r})$}

i.e., we compose
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
with the characteristic session types of all its participants. The cyclic communications of Def.
5.7
ensure that
\Gamma
is live. We can use
\Gamma
to type the composition of a process for
{\sf r}
, of type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
, together with the characteristic processes of the characteristic session types of each participants in
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
: we call such processes the
characteristic session

\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
. This is formalised in Def.
5.9
and Prop.
5.10
below.

Definition 5.9.

For any SI type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
and
{\sf r}\!\not\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
, we define the
characteristic session
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\;=\;\prod_{{\sf p}\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})}\Big({\sf p}\triangleleft{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}_{{\sf p}}\right)\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf p}\triangleleft\varnothing\Big)\qquad\text{where\, $\forall{\sf p}\!\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}):\;{\mathbb{U}}_{{\sf p}}={\tt cyclic}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime},{\sf p},{\sf r})$}

Proposition 5.10.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
be a SI type and
{\sf r}\!\not\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
. Let
Q
be a process such that
\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
. Then, there is a live typing environment
\Gamma
(see (
6
)) such that
\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
.

Crucially, for all
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
such that
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
(e.g., from (
5
) above), we have
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
. Thus, by subsumption,
\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
is also typable with a process of type
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
(
Proposition

5.11
).

Proposition 5.11.

Take any
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,
{\sf r}\!\not\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{T}}^{\prime})
, SI type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}

such that
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
, and
Q
such that
\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
. Then, there is a live
\Gamma
(see (
6
)) such that
\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
.

5.4.
Step 4: Completeness

This final step of our completeness proof encompasses all elements introduced thus far.
(Proofs in
Appendix

C
)

Proposition 5.12.

Let
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
be session types such that
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
. Take any
{\sf r}\!\not\in\!\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{T}}^{\prime})
. Then, there are
{\mathbb{U}}
and
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
with
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\!\in\!\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
and
{\mathbb{U}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
such that:

(1)

\forall Q:\vphantom{\Theta}\vdash Q:{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\,\implies\,\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
for some live
\Gamma
; (by (
6
) and Prop.
5.11
)

(2)

\vdash{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right):{\mathbb{T}}
; (by Prop.
5.6
)

(3)

{\sf r}\triangleleft{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\;\longrightarrow^{*}\;\mathsf{error}
.

Intuitively, we obtain item
3
of
Proposition

5.12
because the characteristic session
\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
expects to interact with a process of type
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
(or a subtype, like
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
); however, when a process that behaves like
{\mathbb{U}}
is inserted, the cyclic communications and/or the expressions
of
\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
(given by Def.
5.5
and
5.7
) are disrupted: this is because
{\mathbb{U}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
, and the (incorrect) message reorderings and mutations allowed by
\not\lesssim
(Table
6
) cause the errors in Table
4
.

We now conclude with our main results.

Theorem 5.13.

The asynchronous multiparty session subtyping
\leqslant
is complete.

Proof.

Direct consequence of
Proposition

5.12
: by taking
{\sf r}
and letting
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
and
P={\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
from its statement, we satisfy item
(2)
of Def.
5.1
.∎

Theorem 5.14.

The asynchronous multiparty session subtyping
\leqslant
is precise.

Proof.

Direct consequence of Theorems
5.2
and
5.13
, which satisfy item
(3)
of Def.
5.1
.∎

6.
Example: Distributed Batch Processing

In this section we illustrate our
subtyping relation by showing the correctness of a messaging optimisation
in a distributed processing scenario.
We adapt a multiparty protocol from the

double-buffering algorithm

(
Huang
et al. 2002
;
Mostrous
et al. 2009
;
Castro-Perez and
Yoshida 2020a
;
Yoshida et al. 2008
)
,
that is widely used, e.g.,
in streaming media applications and computer graphics,
to regulate and speed up asynchronous communication
and data processing.

6.1.
Basic Unoptimised Protocol

(a)

Control
tells
Source
it should send the next batch of data
to
Processor 1
.

(b)

Source
starts sending data to
Processor 1
.
Meanwhile,
Sink
asynchronously tells
Control
it is ready to receive
data from
Processor 1
.

(c)

Processor 1
finishes processing its data
and sends the result to
Sink
; meanwhile,

Control
tells
Source
that it should send the next data
to
Processor 2
.

(d)

Processor 1
has sent all its data to
Sink
;

Source
starts sending data to
Processor 2
,
while
Sink
asynchronously
tells
Control
it is ready to receive data from
Processor 2
.

(e)

Processor 2
is sending its resulting data to
Sink
.

Processor 2
completes the transmission,
we go back to step (
1(a)
).

Figure 1
.
An execution of the basic unoptimised distributed batch processing
protocol.

In a distributed processing system,
two
Batch Processors
are always ready to
receive data from a
Source
, perform some computation, and send
the results to a
Sink
. A
Control
process regulates their
interactions, by telling the Source/Sink where to send/receive new data;
consequently, the Control can influence when the Batch
Processor
s are
running (e.g., all at once, or one a time), and can ensure that the

Source
is not sending too much data (thus overwhelming the Batch

Processor
s and the
Sink
), and the
Sink
is expecting data from an
active
Processor
.

Figure

1
provides an overview of the
communications between the five parties (
Source
,
Sink
,
Control
,
Processor 1
and
Processor 2
) involved in the system.
Their communication protocols can be formalised as multiparty session
types. The types
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{bp1}}
and
T_{\sf{bp2}}
below provide the specification
of the two batch processors. They are very simple:
they recursively read data (of sort
{\mathsf{S}}
) from the
Source
(
\sf{src}
)
and send the processing result to the
Sink
(
\sf{sk}
).

{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{bp1}}\;=\;{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{bp2}}\;=\;\mu\textbf{t}.\sf{src}?\mathit{data}({\mathsf{S}}).\sf{sk}!\mathit{data}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}

We now present the types

{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{src}},{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
, and
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{sk}}
,
which provide the specifications of
Source
,
Control
and
Sink
, respectively:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{src}}&=&\mu\textbf{t}.\texternal\big\{\sf{ctl}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{ctl}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{bp_{1}}!\mathit{data}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\;,\,\sf{ctl}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{ctl}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{bp_{2}}!\mathit{data}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\big\}\\
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{sk}}&=&\mu\textbf{t}.\sf{ctl}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{ctl}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{bp_{1}}?\mathit{data}({\mathsf{S}}).\sf{ctl}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{ctl}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{bp_{2}}?\mathit{data}({\mathsf{S}}).\textbf{t}\\
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}&=&\mu\textbf{t}.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\textbf{t}\end{array}

The above type specifications are summarised as follows:

•

the
Source
(denoted as participant
\sf{src}
)
expects to be told by the
Control
(
\sf{ctl}
)
where to send the next data — either to the
Processor 1
or
Processor 2
(denoted with message labels
\mathit{b}_{1}
and
\mathit{b}_{2}
, respectively).
Then, the
Source
acknowledges
(by replying
\mathit{b}_{1}
or
\mathit{b}_{2}
to the Control)
sends the data to the corresponding
Processor
(
\sf{bp_{1}}
or
\sf{bp_{2}}
), and loops;

•

instead, the
Sink
(denoted as participant
\sf{sk}
)
notifies the
Control
that it is willing to read the output from

Processor 1
(message
\mathit{b}_{1}
), expects an acknowledgement
from
Control
(with the same message
\mathit{b}_{1}
),
and proceeds with reading the data.
Then, it performs a similar sequence of interactions to
notify
Control
and read data from
Processor 2
, and loops;

•

finally, the
Control
(denoted as
\sf{ctl}
)
regulates the interactions of
Source
,
Sink
,
Processor 1
and
Processor 2
,
in a loop where it first
tells Source to send to
Processor 1
, and then waits for Sink
to be ready to receive from
Processor 2
,
and then repeats the above for
Processor 2
.

(a)

Control
tells
Source
it should send the next batch of data
to
Processor 1
.

(b)

Source
starts sending data to
Processor 1
;
asynchronously,
Control
tells
Source
it should
also

send data to
Processor 2
.
Meanwhile,
Sink
asynchronously tells
Control
it is ready to receive
data from
Processor 1
.

(c)

Processor 1
finishes processing its data
and sends the result to
Sink
; meanwhile,

Source
is sending data to
Processor 2
.

(d)

Processor 1
has sent its data to
Sink
;

Source
is still sending to
Processor 2
;

Control
asynchronously tells
Source
to send
data to
Processor 1
;
Sink
asynchronously
tells
Control
it can now receive from
Processor 2
.

(e)

Source
sends data to
Processor 1
,
while
Processor 2
is sending its resulting data to Sink.
After
Processor 2
completes the transmission,
we go back to step (
2(b)
).

Figure 2
.
An execution of the optimised distributed batch processing
protocol. Note that in steps (
1(c)
) and
(
1(e)
) the two batch processors are
active at the same time, and they can perform their computations
in parallel (depending on the time required by the computation,
and the time it takes for the source to send its data.)

6.2.
Optimised Protocol

The type specifications illustrated in
Section

6.1

produce a correct (live) interaction. However, the interaction is also
rather sequential:

•

after
Processor 1
(
\sf{bp_{1}}
) receives its data,

Processor 2
(
\sf{bp_{2}}
) is idle, waiting for data that is only
made available after
Sink
sends a message
\mathit{b}_{1}
to
Control
;

•

later on, while
\sf{bp_{2}}
is processing,
\sf{bp_{1}}

becomes idle, waiting for data that is only made available after

Sink
sends a message
\mathit{b}_{2}
to
Control
.

When implementing the system, it can be appropriate to optimise it by
leveraging asynchronous communications: we can aim at a higher degree
of parallelism for
\sf{bp_{1}}
and
\sf{bp_{2}}
by making
their input data available earlier — without
compromising the overall correctness of the system.

One way to achieve such an optimisation is to
take inspiration from the

double-buffering algorithm

(a version implemented in C is found in
(
Huang
et al. 2002
, Section 3.2)
),
and implement the following
alternative type
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}
for the
Control
that changes the order of some of
the actions of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
:

{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}\;=\;\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\mu\textbf{t}.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\textbf{t}

According to type
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}
,
Control
behaves as follows:

•

first, it signals
Source
that it should send data to

\sf{bp_{1}}
, then to
\sf{bp_{2}}
,
and then enters the loop;

•

inside the loop,
Control
receives the notifications from Source
and Sink, on their intention to send/receive data to/from

\sf{bp_{1}}
; then, the
Control

immediately notifies Source to
send again at
\sf{bp_{1}}
. This refinement allows
Source
to
start sending data earlier, and activate
\sf{bp_{1}}
immediately.

A resulting execution of the protocol is illustrated in

Figure

2
.

We now want to ensure that
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}
represents a correct
optimisation of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
— i.e., if a process implementing
the former is used in a system where a process implementing the latter is
expected, then the optimisation does not introduce deadlocks, orphan
messages, or starvation errors. To this purpose, we show that

{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}
is an asynchronous subtype of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
, by

Definition

3.7
. We take the session trees

{\mathsf{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}}\right)

and
{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}}\right)
, and we define:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}=\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathit{R}}}\right)\\
\text{where\; }{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathit{R}}=\mu\textbf{t}.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\textbf{t}\end{array}

Hence, we have
{\mathsf{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}=\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}
.
We can now prove
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
:
since they are both SISO session types, by
Definition

3.7

it is enough to build a coinductive derivation
showing
{\mathsf{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}\lesssim{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
,
with the SISO refinement rules in
Definition

3.2
. By using the abbreviation

\pi=\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}()
,
and
highlighting
matching pairs of prefixes, we have
the following infinite coinductive derivation:
(notice that the topmost refinement matches the second from the bottom)

{\footnotesize{\mathsf{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}={\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}={\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\pi.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\pi.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\pi.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\pi.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim\pi.{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}()$}}.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}().\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathit{R}}\lesssim\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}().{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}()$}}.\pi.{\mathsf{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}}

Thus, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 6.1.

{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
holds.

The types

{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{bp1}},{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{src}},{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}

and
{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{sk}}
, can be implemented
as processes for
Processor
,
Source
,
Control
and
Sink
, such as:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l}P_{\sf{bp1}}&=&P_{\sf{bp2}}=\mu X.{\sf{src}}?\mathit{data}(x).{\sf{sk}}!\mathit{data}\langle f(x)\rangle.X\\
P_{\sf{src}}&=&\mu X.\sum\big\{\sf{ctl}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{ctl}!\mathit{b}_{1}\langle\rangle.\sf{bp_{1}}!\mathit{data}(\mathit{datum}).X\;,\,\sf{ctl}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{ctl}!\mathit{b}_{2}\langle\rangle.\sf{bp_{2}}!\mathit{data}\langle\mathit{datum}\rangle.X\big\}\\
P_{\sf{ctl}}&=&\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}\langle\rangle.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}\langle\rangle.\mu X.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{1}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{1}\langle\rangle.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{1}\langle\rangle.\sf{src}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}?\mathit{b}_{2}().\sf{sk}!\mathit{b}_{2}\langle\rangle.\sf{src}!\mathit{b}_{2}\langle\rangle.X\\
P_{\sf{sk}}&=&\mu X.{\sf{ctl}}!\mathit{b}_{1}\langle\rangle.{\sf{ctl}}?\mathit{b}_{1}().{\sf{bp_{1}}}?\mathit{data}(y_{1}).{\sf{ctl}}!\mathit{b}_{2}\langle\rangle.{\sf{ctl}}?\mathit{b}_{2}().{\sf{bp_{2}}}?\mathit{data}(y_{2}).X\end{array}

where
f
(used by
P_{\sf{bp1}}
and
P_{\sf{bp2}}
) represents processing of the data received by
\sf{src}
,
and where the variables
x,y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, and objects
f(x)
and
\mathit{datum}
are all of sort
{\mathsf{S}}
.

Proposition 6.2 (Correctness of Optimisation).

Let:

•

\Gamma=\sf{bp_{1}}:(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{bp1}}),\sf{bp_{2}}:(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{bp2}}),\sf{src}:(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{src}}),\sf{ctl}:(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}),\sf{sk}:(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{sk}})
and

•

\mathcal{M}=\sf{bp_{1}}\triangleleft P_{\sf{bp1}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{bp_{1}}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{bp_{2}}\triangleleft P_{\sf{bp2}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{bp_{2}}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{src}\triangleleft P_{\sf{src}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{src}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{ctl}\triangleleft P_{\sf{ctl}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{ctl}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{sk}\triangleleft P_{\sf{sk}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sf{sk}\triangleleft\varnothing

Then, we have that
\Gamma
is live and
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{M}
.
Also,
\mathcal{M}
will never reduce to error.

Observe that
\Gamma
uses the basic unoptimised protocol

{\mathbb{T}}_{\sf{ctl}}
for the
Control
, while
\mathcal{M}
implements the
optimised protocol
{\mathbb{T}}^{*}_{\sf{ctl}}
: the typing holds by
Proposition

6.1
,
while the absence of errors is consequence of
Corollary

4.9
.

7.
Additional Preciseness Results

We now use our main result (
Theorem

5.14
) to prove two additional results:
our subtyping
\leqslant
is
denotationally precise
(
Section

7.1
)
and also
precise wrt. liveness
(
Section

7.2
).

7.1.
Denotational Preciseness of Subtyping

The approach to preciseness of subtyping described in the previous sections
is nowadays dubbed
operational preciseness
.
In prior work, the canonical approach to preciseness of subtyping
for a given calculus has been
denotational
:
a type
{\mathbb{T}}
is interpreted (with notation
[\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]
)
as the set that describes the meaning of
{\mathbb{T}}
, according
to denotations of the expressions (terms, processes,
etc.
)
of the calculus.
E.g.,
\lambda
-calculus types are usually interpreted as
subsets of the domains of
\lambda
-models
(
Barendregt et al. 1983
;
Hindley 1983
)
.
With this approach, subtyping is interpreted as set-theoretic inclusion.

Under this denotational viewpoint,
a subtyping
\unlhd
is sound when
{\mathbb{T}}\unlhd{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\ \text{ \,implies\, }\ [\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\subseteq[\![{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}]\!]
, and complete when
[\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\subseteq[\![{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}]\!]\ \text{ \,implies\, }\ {\mathbb{T}}\unlhd{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
.
Hence, a subtyping
\unlhd
is precise when:

{\mathbb{T}}\unlhd{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}\ \text{ \;if and only if\; }\ [\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\subseteq[\![{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}]\!]

This notion is nowadays dubbed
denotational preciseness
.

We now adapt the denotational approach to our setting.
Let us interpret a session type
{\mathbb{T}}
as the set of closed processes typed by
{\mathbb{T}}
, i.e.

[\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\;=\;\{P\penalty\ \mid\vphantom{x}\vdash P:{\mathbb{T}}\}

We now show that our subtyping is denotationally precise. The denotational soundness follows from the subsumption rule
[t-sub]
(
Table

5
) and the interpretation of subtyping as set-theoretic inclusion. In order to prove that denotational completeness holds, we show a more general result (along the lines of
Ghilezan et al. 2019
): by leveraging characteristic processes and sessions, we show that operational completeness implies denotational completeness.

Theorem 7.1.

The multiparty asynchronous subtyping
\leqslant
is denotationally complete.

Proof.

We proceed by contradiction.
Suppose that denotational completeness does
not
hold, i.e.,
there are
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
such that

[\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\subseteq[\![{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}]\!]
but
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
.

•

From
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, by Step 1 (
Section

5.1
, eq. (
5
)), there are
{\mathbb{U}}
and
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
satisfying
{\mathbb{U}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{U}}}\right)\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SO}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\right)\in\llbracket\operatorname{\mathcal{T}}\!\left({{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}}\right)\rrbracket_{\text{\tiny\sf{SI}}}
. Then by Step 4 (
Proposition

5.12
(3)) we have:

(7)

\displaystyle{\sf r}\triangleleft{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\;\longrightarrow^{*}\;\mathsf{error}

where
\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
, (with
{\sf r}\not\in\mathtt{pt}({\mathbb{V}}^{\prime})
) is the characteristic
session of Step 3 (
Definition

5.9
) and
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)
is the characteristic process of
{\mathbb{U}}
of Step
2 (
Definition

5.5
).

•

By Step 2 (
Proposition

5.6
), the characteristic process of the above
{\mathbb{U}}
is typable by
{\mathbb{T}}
, i.e., we have
\vdash{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right):{\mathbb{T}}
; therefore, from
[\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\subseteq[\![{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}]\!]
we also have
\vdash{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right):{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
. Hence, by Step 3 (
Definition

5.9
,
Proposition

5.11
) for the above
{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}
the characteristic session
\mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}
is typable if composed with
{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right):{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, i.e.:

(8)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash{\sf r}\triangleleft{\mathcal{P}}\!\left({\mathbb{U}}\right)\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\sf r}\triangleleft\varnothing\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{{\sf r},{\mathbb{V}}^{\prime}}\quad\mbox{for some live}\;\Gamma

But then, (
8
) and (
7
)
contradict the soundness of the type system (
Theorem

4.8
).
Therefore, we conclude that for all
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,

[\![{\mathbb{T}}]\!]\subseteq[\![{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}]\!]
implies
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,
which is the thesis.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain the denotational preciseness of subtyping.

Theorem 7.2 (Denotational preciseness).

The subtyping relation is denotationally precise.

7.2.
Preciseness of Subtyping wrt. Liveness

Our results also provide a stepping stone to show that
that
our multiparty asynchronous subtyping is precise wrt. liveness
(
Definition

4.4
),
as formalised in
Theorem

7.3
below.

Notably, this result can be lifted to the realm of
Communicating Finite-State Machines (CFSMs)
(
Brand and
Zafiropulo 1983
)
,
by the following interpretation.
Communicating session automata (CSA)

(introduced by
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2012
) are a class of CFSMs
with a finite control based on internal/external choices,
corresponding to a session type; a typing environment
\Gamma
corresponds to a system of CSA,
and reduces with the same semantics (
Definition

4.3
).
Therefore, our notion of typing environment liveness (
Definition

4.4
)
guarantees deadlock-freedom, orphan message-freedom, and starvation-freedom
of the corresponding system of CSA — similarly to the notion of
\infty
-multiparty compatibility

between CSA, by
Lange and Yoshida 2019
. Correspondingly, by
Theorem

7.3
,
our subtyping
\leqslant
is a sound and complete preorder allowing to refine any live system of CSA
(by replacing one or more automata), while preserving the overall liveness of the system.

To state the result, we adopt the notation

\Gamma\!\left\{{{\sf p}}\mapsto{(\sigma,{\mathbb{T}})}\right\}
to represent the typing environment obtained from
\Gamma
by replacing the entry for
{\sf p}
with
(\sigma,{\mathbb{T}})
.

Theorem 7.3.

For all session types
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, multiparty asynchronous subtyping
\leqslant
is:

sound wrt. liveness::

if
{\mathbb{T}}\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,  then
\forall\Gamma
with
\Gamma({\sf r})\!=\!(\epsilon,\!{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime})
, when
\Gamma
is live,
\Gamma\!\left\{{{\sf r}\!}\mapsto{\!(\epsilon,\!{\mathbb{T}})}\right\}
is live;

complete wrt. liveness::

if
{\mathbb{T}}\not\leqslant{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
,  then
\exists\Gamma
live with
\Gamma({\sf r})\!=\!(\epsilon,\!{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime})
, but
\Gamma\!\left\{{{\sf r}\!}\mapsto{\!(\epsilon,\!{\mathbb{T}})}\right\}
is not live.

Proof.

Soundness of
\leqslant
follows by
Lemma

4.7
.
Completeness, instead, descends from
Proposition

5.12
: for any
{\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathbb{T}}\!\not\leqslant\!{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}
, we can build a live typing context
\Gamma
(see (
6
)) with
\Gamma({\sf r})\!=\!(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime})
and cyclic communications (item
1
). Observe that the environment
\Gamma\!\left\{{{\sf r}}\mapsto{(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}})}\right\}
types the session of
Proposition

5.12
(
3
), that reduces to
\mathsf{error}
: hence, by the contrapositive of
Corollary

4.9
, we conclude that
\Gamma\!\left\{{{\sf r}}\mapsto{(\epsilon,{\mathbb{T}})}\right\}
is not live.
∎

8.
Related Work, Future Work, and Conclusion

Precise Subtyping for
\lambda
-Calculus and Semantic Subtyping

The notion of preciseness has been adopted
as a criterion to justify the canonicity of subtyping relations,
in the context of both functional and concurrent calculi.
Operational preciseness of subtyping was first introduced by
Blackburn et al. 2012
(and later published
in
(
Ligatti
et al. 2017
)
), and applied to
\lambda
-calculus with iso-recursive types. Later,
Dezani-Ciancaglini and Ghilezan 2014
adapted the idea of
Blackburn et al. 2012
to the setting of the concurrent
\lambda
-calculus with intersection and union types by
Dezani-Ciancaglini et al. 1998
, and proved both operational
and denotational preciseness. In the context of the
\lambda
-calculus,
a similar framework,
semantic subtyping
, was proposed by

Castagna and
Frisch 2005
: each type
T
is interpreted as the
set of values having type
T
, and subtyping is defined as subset inclusion between type interpretations. This gives a precise subtyping as long as the calculus
allows to operationally distinguish values of different types.
Semantic subtyping was also studied
by
Castagna
et al. 2008
(for a
\pi
-calculus with a patterned input and IO-types), and by
Castagna et al. 2009a
(for a
\pi
-calculus with binary session types); in both works, types include union, intersection and negation.
Semantic subtyping is precise for the
calculi of
(
Castagna
et al. 2008
;
Castagna et al. 2009a
;
Frisch
et al. 2008
)
: this is due to the type case constructor in
(
Frisch
et al. 2008
)
, and to the blocking of inputs for values of “wrong” types in
(
Castagna
et al. 2008
;
Castagna et al. 2009a
)
.

Precise Subtyping for Session Types

In the context of
binary
session types, the first general formulation of precise subtyping (synchronous and asynchronous) is given by

Chen et al. 2017
, for a
\pi
-calculus typed by assigning session types to channels (as in the work by
Honda
et al. 1998
). The first result by

Chen et al. 2017
is that the well-known
branching-selection subtyping
(
Gay and Hole 2005
;
Demangeon and
Honda 2011
)
is sound and complete for the
synchronous
binary session
\pi
- calculus.

Chen et al. 2017
also examine an
asynchronous
binary session
\pi
-calculus, and introduce a subtyping relation (restricting the subtyping for the higher-order
\pi
-calculus by
Mostrous and
Yoshida 2015
) that is also proved precise.
Our results imply that the binary session subtyping of
Chen et al. 2017

(without delegation) is a subset of our multiparty subtyping, hence
the subtyping of
Chen et al. 2017
is
expressible via tree decomposition.
We compare our subtyping and theirs
(specifically, the use
n
-hole contexts)
in
Remark

3.6
;
further, we prove
Example

3.10
via tree decomposition
— and such a proof would be more difficult
with the rules of
Chen et al. 2017
.

In the context of
multiparty
session types,
Ghilezan et al. 2019

adopt an approach similar to
Chen et al. 2017
to prove that the
synchronous
multiparty extension of binary session subtyping
(
Gay and Hole 2005
)
is sound and complete, hence precise.

Our subtyping becomes equivalent to
Ghilezan et al. 2019
by removing rules

[
ref-
\mathcal{A}
]
and
[
ref-
\mathcal{B}
]

from
Definition

3.2
;
however, such a formulation would not be much simpler than the one by

Ghilezan et al. 2019
, nor would simplify its algorithm:
lacking asynchrony, their subtyping is already quite simple, and decidable.

An
asynchronous
subtyping relation for multiparty session types was proposed by
Mostrous
et al. 2009
;
notably, the paper claims that the relation is decidable,
but this was later disproved by
Bravetti
et al. 2018

(see next paragraph). The main crucial difference between our subtyping
and the one by
Mostrous
et al. 2009

is that the latter is
unsound
in our setting:
it does not guarantee orphan message freedom,
hence it accepts processes that reduce to
\mathsf{error}

by rule
[
err-ophn
]
in
Table

4

(see the counterexamples in
(
Chen et al. 2017
, page 46)
,
also applicable in the multiparty setting).
A recent work by
Horne 2020

introduces a novel formalisation of subtyping
for
synchronous
multiparty session types
equipped with parallel composition.
Horne’s work does not address the problem of precise subtyping,
and its extension to asynchronous communication seems non-trivial:
in fact, the subtyping appears to be unsound in our setting, because (as in
Mostrous
et al. 2009
)
it focuses on deadlock-freedom, and does not address, e.g.,
orphan message freedom.
Studying such extensions and issues in the setting of
Horne 2020

can lead to intriguing future work.

Session Types and Communicating Automata

Asynchronous session subtyping was shown to be undecidable, even for
binary sessions, by
Lange and Yoshida 2017
and
Bravetti
et al. 2018
,
by leveraging a correspondence between session types and
communicating automata theories
— a correspondence first established by
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2012
with the notion of
session automata

based on Communicating Finite-State Machines
(
Brand and
Zafiropulo 1983
)
.
This undecidability result prompted the research on
various limited classes of (binary)
session types for which asynchronous subtyping is decidable

(
Bravetti et al. 2019a
;
Bravetti
et al. 2018
;
Lange and Yoshida 2017
)
:
for example, if we have binary session types without choices
(which, in our setting, corresponds to single-input, single-output types
interacting with a single other participant),
then asynchronous subtyping becomes decidable.
-The aim of our paper
is
not
finding a decidable approximation of asynchronous
multiparty
session subtyping, but finding a canonical,
precise
subtyping.
Interestingly, our SISO decomposition technique leads to:

(1)

intuitive but general refinement rules (see Example
3.10
, where
\leqslant
proves an example not supported by the subtyping algorithm in

(
Bravetti et al. 2019a
)
); and

(2)

preciseness of
\leqslant
wrt. liveness (Theorem
7.3
)
which is directly usable to define
the precise multiparty asynchronous refinement relation
wrt. liveness in communicating session automata
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2012
;
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
.

Future Work

We plan to investigate
precise subtyping for richer multiparty session
\pi
-calculi, e.g. with multiple session initiations and delegation,

with a formalisation similar to
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
.
We expect that, even in this richer setting,
our subtyping relation will remain the same,
and its preciseness will be proved in the same way
— except that session types will support session types as payloads,
and Table
6

and Def.
5.5
will have more cases.
This can be observed in the work by
Chen et al. 2017
: they support multiple sessions, process spawning, delegation — and yet, such features don’t impact the precise subtyping relation (except for having session types as payloads).

We also plan to study the precise subtyping for
typing environment properties
other than our liveness (Def.
4.4
).
For example, we may consider a weaker deadlock freedom property,
which can be sufficient for correct session typing
(as shown by
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
)
as long as the session calculus does
not
include error reductions
in case of starvation or orphan messages
(rules
[
err-strv
]
and
[
err-ophn
]

in
Table

4
).
We expect that, if we lift the liveness requirement,
the resulting precise subtyping relation will be
somewhat surprising and counter-intuitive, and harder to formulate.
For example: due to the lack of orphan message errors,
subtyping should allow processes forget their inputs in some situations,
hence we should have, e.g.,
\mathtt{end}\leqslant{\sf p}?\ell(\mathtt{nat}).\mathtt{end}
.

Another compelling topic for future work
is finding non-trivial decidable approximations of our multiparty
asynchronous subtyping relation. As remarked above,
the relation is inherently undecidable
— hence type-checking is also undecidable,
because rule
[
t-sub
]
(
Table

5
) is undecidable.
Our relation is trivially decidable if restricted to
non-recursive types.
The known decidable fragments of binary asynchronous subtyping

(
Bravetti et al. 2019a
;
Bravetti
et al. 2018
;
Lange and Yoshida 2017
)
are sound wrt. our relation
— but they are also limited to two-party sessions,
and we suspect that they may become undecidable if naively
generalised to multiparty sessions.
If a decidable approximation of asynchronous subtyping is adopted
for rule
[
t-sub
]
(
Table

5
),
then type-chcecking becomes decidable.

Conclusion

Unlike this paper, no other published work addresses precise
asynchronous

multiparty
session subtyping. A main challenge was the exact formalisation of the subtyping itself, which must satisfy many
desiderata
: it must capture a wide variety of input/output
reorderings performed by different participants,
without being too strict (otherwise, completeness is lost) nor too lax (otherwise, soundness is lost); moreover, its definition must not be overly complex to understand, and tractable in proofs. We achieved these
desiderata
with our novel approach, based on SISO tree decomposition and refinement,
which yields a simpler subtyping definition than

(
Chen et al. 2017
)
(see Remark
3.6
).
Moreover,
our results are much more general than
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
: by using live typing environments (Def.
4.4
), we are not limited to sessions that match some global type; our results are also stronger, as we prove soundness wrt. a wider range of errors (see Table
4
).
